Area distance.
The area distance is defined according to the idea

As a formal definition for , in terms of the extremal angular diameter distances and as functions of affine distance , we use the equation
indeed relates, for a bundle with vertex at the observer, the cross-sectional area at the source to the opening solid angle at the observer. Such a bundle has a caustic point exactly at those points where . The area distance is often called “angular diameter distance” although, as indicated by Equation (41), the name “averaged angular diameter distance” would be more appropriate. Just as and , the area distance depends on the 4-velocity of the observer but not on the 4-velocity of the light source. The area distance is observable for a light source whose true size is known (or can be reasonably estimated). It is sometimes convenient to introduce the magnification or amplification factor
The absolute value of determines the area distance, and the sign of determines the parity. In Minkowski spacetime, and, thus, . Hence, means that a (small but extended) light source at affine distance subtends a larger solid angle on the observer’s sky than a light source of the same size at the same affine distance in Minkowski spacetime. Note that in a multiple-imaging situation the individual images may have different affine distances. Thus, the relative magnification factor of two images is not directly observable. This is an important difference to the magnification factor that is used in the quasi-Newtonian approximation formalism of lensing. The latter is defined by comparison with an “unlensed image” (see, e.g., [298]), a notion that makes sense only if the metric is viewed as a perturbation of some “background” metric. One can derive a differential equation for the area distance (or, equivalently, for the magnification factor) as a function of affine distance in the following way. On every parameter interval where has no zeros, the real part of Equation (27) shows that the area distance is related to the expansion by
Insertion into the Sachs equation (25) for gives the focusing equation
Between the vertex at and the first conjugate point (caustic point), is determined by Equation (44) and the initial conditions
The Ricci term in Equation (44) is non-negative if Einstein’s field equation holds and if the energy density is non-negative for all observers (“weak energy condition”). Then Equations (44, 45) imply that
i.e., , for all between the vertex at and the first conjugate point. In Minkowski spacetime, Equation (46) holds with equality. Hence, Equation (46) says that the gravitational field has a focusing, as opposed to a defocusing, effect. This is sometimes called the focusing theorem.

Corrected luminosity distance.
The idea of defining distance measures in terms of bundle cross-sections dates back to Tolman [322] and Whittaker [351]. Originally, this idea was applied not to bundles with vertex at the observer but rather to bundles with vertex at the light source. The resulting analogue of the area distance is the so-called corrected luminosity distance . It relates, for a bundle with vertex at the light source, the cross-sectional area at the observer to the opening solid angle at the light source. Owing to Etherington’s reciprocity law (35), area distance and corrected luminosity distance are related by

The redshift factor has its origin in the fact that the definition of refers to an affine parametrization adapted to , and the definition of refers to an affine parametrization adapted to . While depends on but not on , depends on but not on .

Luminosity distance.
The physical meaning of the corrected luminosity distance is most easily understood in the photon picture. For photons isotropically emitted from a light source, the percentage that hit a prescribed area at the observer is proportional to . As the energy of each photon undergoes a redshift, the energy flux at the observer is proportional to , where

Thus, is the relevant quantity for calculating the luminosity (apparent brightness) of pointlike light sources (see Equation (52)). For this reason is called the (uncorrected) luminosity distance. The observation that the purely geometric quantity must be modified by an additional redshift factor to give the energy flux is due to Walker [342]. depends on the 4-velocity of the observer and of the 4-velocity of the light source. and can be viewed as functions of the observational coordinates if a vector field with has been distinguished, one integral curve of is chosen as the observer, and the other integral curves of are chosen as the light sources. In that case Equation (38) implies that not only but also and are of the form . Thus, near the observer all three distance measures coincide with Euclidean distance in the observer’s rest space.

Parallax distance.
In an arbitrary spacetime, we fix an observation event and the observer’s 4-velocity . We consider a past-oriented lightlike geodesic parametrized by affine distance, and . To a light source passing through the event we assign the (averaged) parallax distance , where is the expansion of an infinitesimally thin bundle with vertex at . This definition follows [171]. Its relevance in view of cosmology was discussed in detail by Rosquist [288]. can be measured by performing the standard trigonometric parallax method of elementary Euclidean geometry, with the observer at and an assistant observer at the perimeter of the bundle, and then averaging over all possible positions of the assistant. Note that the method refers to a bundle with vertex at the light source, i.e., to light rays that leave the light source simultaneously. (Averaging is not necessary if this bundle is circular.) depends on the 4-velocity of the observer but not on the 4-velocity of the light source. To within first-order approximation near the observer it coincides with affine distance (recall Equation (32)). For the potential obervational relevance of see [288], and [298], p. 509.

In view of lensing, , , and are the most important distance measures because they are related to image distortion (see Section 2.5) and to the brightness of images (see Section 2.6). In spacetimes with many symmetries, these quantities can be explicitly calculated (see Section 4.1 for conformally flat spactimes, and Section 4.3 for spherically symmetric static spacetimes). This is impossible in a spacetime without symmetries, in particular in a realistic cosmological model with inhomogeneities (“clumpy universe”). Following Kristian and Sachs [189], one often uses series expansions with respect to . For statistical considerations one may work with the focusing equation in a Friedmann–Robertson–Walker spacetime with average density (see Section 4.1), or with a heuristically modified focusing equation taking clumps into account. The latter leads to the so-called Dyer–Roeder distance [8687] which is discussed in several text-books (see, e.g., [298]). (For pre-Dyer–Roeder papers on optics in cosmological models with inhomogeneities, see the historical notes in [173].) As overdensities have a focusing and underdensities have a defocusing effect, it is widely believed (following [344]) that after averaging over sufficiently large angular scales the Friedmann–Robertson–Walker calculation gives the correct distance-redshift relation. However, it was argued by Ellis, Bassett, and Dunsby [99] that caustics produced by the lensing effect of overdensities lead to a systematic bias towards smaller angular sizes (“shrinking”). For a spherically symmetric inhomogeneity, the effect on the distance-redshift relation can be calculated analytically [231]. For thorough discussions of light propagation in a clumpy universe also see Pyne and Birkinshaw [284], and Holz and Wald [160].<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a function `extract_keywords(text)` that identifies the top 5 most frequent words in a given text, excluding common stop words such as "the", "and", "is", etc. The function should return a list of tuples, each containing a word and its frequency count. If there are fewer than 5 unique words after filtering out stop words, return all unique words with their frequency counts.

**Input:**
- A string `text` (1 ≤ len(text) ≤ 10,000) containing English text.

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains a word and its frequency count. The list should be sorted by frequency in descending order. If frequencies are the same, the words should be sorted lexicographically.

**Sample Input:**
```
"Python is a high-level, interpreted, general-purpose programming language. Its design philosophy emphasizes code readability with the use of significant indentation. Python is dynamically typed and garbage-collected. It supports multiple programming paradigms, including structured (particularly procedural), object-oriented, and functional programming."
```

**Sample Output:**
```
[('python', 2), ('programming', 2), ('language', 1), ('highlevel', 1), ('interpreted', 1)]
```

```python
from collections import Counter
import re
from nltk.corpus import stopwords
import nltk

nltk.download('stopwords')  # Download stopwords if not already downloaded

def extract_keywords(text):
    """
    Extracts the top 5 most common words from a given text, excluding common stop words.
    
    Parameters:
    text (str): The input text from which to extract keywords.
    
    Returns:
    list of tuples: A list of tuples, each containing a word and its frequency count.
    """
    # List of common stop words
    stop_words = set(stopwords.words("english"))
    
    # Clean text: remove punctuation and make lowercase
    words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', text.lower())
    
    # Filter out stop words
    filtered_words = [word for word in words if word not in stop_words]
    
    # Count the frequency of each word
    word_counts = Counter(filtered_words)
    
    # Get the top 5 most common words
    top_keywords = word_counts.most_common(5)
    
    return top_keywords

# Example usage
text = "Python is a high-level, interpreted, general-purpose programming language. Its design philosophy emphasizes code readability with the use of significant indentation. Python is dynamically typed and garbage-collected. It supports multiple programming paradigms, including structured (particularly procedural), object-oriented, and functional programming."
print(extract_keywords(text))

Thus, the alternating sum evaluates to:

\[
\sum_{k=0}^n (-1)^k \binom{m}{n-k} \binom{m}{k} = \begin{cases} 
(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}} \binom{m}{n/2} & \text{if } n \text{ is even} \\
0 & \text{if } n \text{ is odd}
\end{cases}.
\]

Therefore, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{ \begin{cases} 
(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}} \binom{m}{n/2} & \text{if } n \text{ is even} \\
0 & \text{if } n \text{ is odd}
\end{cases} }

# Continuous wave

Overview
A continuous wave or continuous waveform (CW) is an electromagnetic wave of constant amplitude
Amplitude
Amplitude is the magnitude of change in the oscillating variable with each oscillation within an oscillating system. For example, sound waves in air are oscillations in atmospheric pressure and their amplitudes are proportional to the change in pressure during one oscillation...

and frequency
Frequency
Frequency is the number of occurrences of a repeating event per unit time. It is also referred to as temporal frequency.The period is the duration of one cycle in a repeating event, so the period is the reciprocal of the frequency...

; and in mathematical analysis
Mathematical analysis
Mathematical analysis, which mathematicians refer to simply as analysis, has its beginnings in the rigorous formulation of infinitesimal calculus. It is a branch of pure mathematics that includes the theories of differentiation, integration and measure, limits, infinite series, and analytic functions...

, of infinite duration. Continuous wave is also the name given to an early method of radio
Radio is the transmission of signals through free space by modulation of electromagnetic waves with frequencies below those of visible light. Electromagnetic radiation travels by means of oscillating electromagnetic fields that pass through the air and the vacuum of space...

transmission
Transmission (telecommunications)
Transmission, in telecommunications, is the process of sending, propagating and receiving an analogue or digital information signal over a physical point-to-point or point-to-multipoint transmission medium, either wired, optical fiber or wireless...

, in which a carrier wave
Carrier wave
In telecommunications, a carrier wave or carrier is a waveform that is modulated with an input signal for the purpose of conveying information. This carrier wave is usually a much higher frequency than the input signal...

is switched on and off. Information
Information
Information in its most restricted technical sense is a message or collection of messages that consists of an ordered sequence of symbols, or it is the meaning that can be interpreted from such a message or collection of messages. Information can be recorded or transmitted. It can be recorded as...

is carried in the varying duration of the on and off periods
On-off keying
On-off keying the simplest form of amplitude-shift keying modulation that represents digital data as the presence or absence of a carrier wave. In its simplest form, the presence of a carrier for a specific duration represents a binary one, while its absence for the same duration represents a...

of the signal, for example by Morse code
Morse code
Morse code is a method of transmitting textual information as a series of on-off tones, lights, or clicks that can be directly understood by a skilled listener or observer without special equipment...

in early radio. In early wireless telegraphy
Wireless telegraphy
Wireless telegraphy is a historical term used today to apply to early radio telegraph communications techniques and practices, particularly those used during the first three decades of radio before the term radio came into use....

radio transmission, CW waves were also known as "undamped waves", to distinguish this method from damped wave
Damped wave
A damped wave is a wave whose amplitude of oscillation decreases with time, eventually going to zero. This term also refers to an early method of radio transmission produced by spark gap transmitters, which consisted of a series of damped electromagnetic waves...

transmission.

Thus it is commonly understood<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a RESTful API using Flask to manage a collection of books stored in a SQLite database. The API should support the following operations:

- **Create**: Add a new book to the collection.
- **Read**: Retrieve all books or a specific book by its ID.
- **Update**: Modify the details of an existing book.
- **Delete**: Remove a book from the collection.

The `books` table in the SQLite database should have the following schema:
- `book_id`: An integer that uniquely identifies each book (primary key).
- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `published_year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.

Your API should handle input validation and return appropriate HTTP status codes and JSON responses. Implement the necessary routes to support these operations.

**Sample Input/Output:**

1. **Create a Book**
   - **Request**: `POST /books` with JSON body `{"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1949}`
   - **Response**: `201 Created` with JSON body `{"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1949}`

2. **Retrieve All Books**
   - **Request**: `GET /books`
   - **Response**: `200 OK` with JSON body `[{"book_id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1949}]`

3. **Retrieve a Specific Book**
   - **Request**: `GET /books/1`
   - **Response**: `200 OK` with JSON body `{"book_id": 1, "title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1949}`

4. **Update a Book**
   - **Request**: `PUT /books/1` with JSON body `{"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1945}`
   - **Response**: `200 OK` with JSON body `{"title": "Animal Farm", "author": "George Orwell", "published_year": 1945}`

5. **Delete a Book**
   - **Request**: `DELETE /books/1`
   - **Response**: `204 No Content`

```python
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import sqlite3
from marshmallow import Schema, fields, ValidationError

app = Flask(__name__)

def get_db_connection():
    """Establish a connection to the SQLite database."""
    conn = sqlite3.connect('books.db')
    conn.row_factory = sqlite3.Row
    return conn

def init_db():
    """Initialize the books table if it does not exist."""
    conn = get_db_connection()
    cursor = conn.cursor()
    cursor.execute('''
        CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS books (
            book_id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
            title TEXT NOT NULL,
            author TEXT NOT NULL,
            published_year INTEGER NOT NULL
        )
    ''')
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

init_db()

class BookSchema(Schema):
    """Schema for validating book data."""
    title = fields.String(required=True)
    author = fields.String(required=True)
    published_year = fields.Integer(required=True)

book_schema = BookSchema()
books_schema = BookSchema(many=True)

@app.route('/books', methods=['POST'])
def add_book():
    """Add a new book to the database."""
    try:
        new_book = book_schema.load(request.get_json())
    except ValidationError as err:
        return jsonify(err.messages), 400

    conn = get_db_connection()
    cursor = conn.cursor()
    sql = ''' INSERT INTO books(title, author, published_year)
              VALUES(?, ?, ?) '''
    cursor.execute(sql, (new_book['title'], new_book['author'], new_book['published_year']))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

    return jsonify(new_book), 201

@app.route('/books', methods=['GET'])
def get_books():
    """Retrieve all books from the database."""
    conn = get_db_connection()
    cursor = conn.cursor()
    cursor.execute('SELECT * FROM books')
    books = cursor.fetchall()
    conn.close()

    return jsonify([dict(book) for book in books])

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['GET'])
def get_book(book_id):
    """Retrieve a specific book by its ID."""
    conn = get_db_connection()
    cursor = conn.cursor()
    cursor.execute('SELECT * FROM books WHERE book_id = ?', (book_id,))
    book = cursor.fetchone()
    conn.close()

    if book is None:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Book not found'}), 404

    return jsonify(dict(book))

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['PUT'])
def update_book(book_id):
    """Update the details of an existing book."""
    try:
        updated_book = book_schema.load(request.get_json())
    except ValidationError as err:
        return jsonify(err.messages), 400

    conn = get_db_connection()
    cursor = conn.cursor()
    sql = ''' UPDATE books SET
              title = ?,
              author = ?,
              published_year = ?
              WHERE book_id = ? '''
    cursor.execute(sql, (updated_book['title'], updated_book['author'], updated_book['published_year'], book_id))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

    if cursor.rowcount == 0:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Book not found'}), 404

    return jsonify(updated_book), 200

@app.route('/books/<int:book_id>', methods=['DELETE'])
def delete_book(book_id):
    """Delete a book from the database."""
    conn = get_db_connection()
    cursor = conn.cursor()
    sql = 'DELETE FROM books WHERE book_id = ?'
    cursor.execute(sql, (book_id,))
    conn.commit()
    conn.close()

    if cursor.rowcount == 0:
        return jsonify({'error': 'Book not found'}), 404

    return '', 204

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)

+# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-
+import logging
+from datetime import date
+
+LOG = logging.getLogger(__name__)
+
+def general_metrics(db):
+    """Create an object with database metrics
+
+    Args:
+        db(pymongo.database.Database)
+
+    Returns:
+        metrics(dict): According to the MME API it should be a dictionary like this:
+            {
+                "metrics": {
+                    "numberOfCases": 0,
+                    "numberOfSubmitters": 0,
+                    "numberOfGenes": 0,
+                    "numberOfUniqueGenes": 0,
+                    "numberOfVariants": 0,
+                    "numberOfUniqueVariants": 0,
+                    "numberOfFeatures": 0,
+                    "numberOfUniqueFeatures": 0,
+                    "numberOfFeatureSets": 0, # endpoint is not returning this, at the moment
+                    "numberOfUniqueGenesMatched": 0,
+                    "numberOfCasesWithDiagnosis": 0,
+                    "numberOfRequestsReceived": 0,
+                    "numberOfPotentialMatchesSent": 0,
+                    "dateGenerated": "2017-08-24",
+
+                },
+                "disclaimer": "Disclaimer text...",
+                "terms": "Terms text..."
+            }
+    """
+    # get gene/occurrence for all genes in db
+    n_genes = 0
+    gene_occurrs = item_occurrence(db, 'genomicFeatures', 'genomicFeatures.gene', 'genomicFeatures.gene.id')
+    for gene_count in gene_occurrs:
+        n_genes += gene_count['count']
+
+    # get numberOfUniqueVariants/occurrence for all variants in db
+    variant_occurr = item_occurrence(db, 'genomicFeatures', 'genomicFeatures.variant', 'genomicFeatures.variant')
+    n_vars = 0
+    for var in variant_occurr:
+        n_vars += var.get('count')
+
+    # get feature/occurrence for all features in db
+    n_feat = 0
+    feat_occurr = item_occurrence(db, 'features', 'features.id')
+    for feat in feat_occurr:
+        n_feat += feat.get('count')
+
+    # include in unique_gene_matches only matches actively returned by the server (internal)
+    match_type = {'match_type':'internal'}
+    unique_gene_matches = db.matches.distinct('results.patients.patient.genomicFeatures.gene', match_type)
+
+    metrics = {
+        'numberOfCases' : db.patients.find().count(),
+        'numberOfSubmitters' : len(db.patients.distinct('contact.href')),
+        'numberOfGenes' : n_genes,
+        'numberOfUniqueGenes': len(db.patients.distinct('genomicFeatures.gene')),
+        'numberOfVariants' : n_vars,
+        'numberOfUniqueVariants' : len(db.patients.distinct('genomicFeatures.variant')),
+        'numberOfFeatures' : n_feat,
+        'numberOfUniqueFeatures' : len(db.patients.distinct('features.id')),
+        'numberOfUniqueGenesMatched' : len(unique_gene_matches),
+        'numberOfCasesWithDiagnosis' : db.patients.find({'disorders': {'$exists': True, '$ne' : []} }).count(),
+        'numberOfRequestsReceived' : db.matches.find({'match_type':'internal'}).count(),
+        'numberOfPotentialMatchesSent' : db.matches.find({'match_type':'internal', 'has_matches': True}).count(),
+        'dateGenerated' : str(date.today())
+    }
+    return metrics
+
+
+def item_occurrence(db, unw1, group, unw2=None):
+    """Get a list of item/occurrence in patient collection
+
+    Args:
+        db(pymongo.database.Database)
+        unw1(string): first nested unwind item
+        group(string): item to group results by
+        unw2(string): second nested unwind item # none if nested level is missing
+
+    Returns:
+        item_occurr(list) example: [{'id':'item_obj', 'count': item_occurrence}, ..]
+    """
+    # create query pipeline
+    pipeline = [{"$unwind": ''.join(['$',unw1])}]
+    if unw2:
+        pipeline.append({"$unwind": ''.join(['$',unw2])})
+    pipeline.append({"$group": {"_id": ''.join(['$',group]), "count": {"$sum": 1}}})
+    item_occurr = list(db.patients.aggregate(pipeline))
+    return item_occurr
<#sep#> temporal frequency.The period is the duration of one cycle in a repeating event, so the period is the reciprocal of the frequency...

. Mathematically, the extremely wideband excitation provided by the spark gap was bandpass filtered by the self-oscillating antenna side circuit, which because of its simple construction, unfortunately also had a relatively broad and badly controlled filter characteristic.

This motivated efforts to produce radio frequency oscillations that decayed more slowly. Strictly speaking an unmodulated, continuous carrier has no bandwidth and cannot interfere with signals at other frequencies, but on the other hand conveys no information either. Thus it is commonly understood that the act of keying the carrier on and off is necessary. However, in order to bring the bandwidth of the resulting signal under control, the buildup and decay of the radio frequency envelope needed to be slower than in the early spark gap implementations.

When this is done, the spectrum of the signal approaches that of a continuous sinusoidal oscillation, while temporally its amplitude
Amplitude
Amplitude is the magnitude of change in the oscillating variable with each oscillation within an oscillating system. For example, sound waves in air are oscillations in atmospheric pressure and their amplitudes are proportional to the change in pressure during one oscillation...

does vary between zero and full carrier strength. As such, the resulting narrower bandwidth mode of operation is to this day described as "continuous wave". The resulting signal allows many radio stations to share a given band of frequencies without noticeable mutual interference.

The first transmitters capable of producing continuous wave, the Alexanderson alternator
Alexanderson alternator
An Alexanderson alternator is a rotating machine invented by Ernst Alexanderson in 1904 for the generation of high frequency alternating current up to 100 kHz, for use as a radio transmitter...

and vacuum tube
Vacuum tube
In electronics, a vacuum tube, electron tube , or thermionic valve , reduced to simply "tube" or "valve" in everyday parlance, is a device that relies on the flow of electric current through a vacuum...

oscillators, became widely available after World War I
World War I
World War I , which was predominantly called the World War or the Great War from its occurrence until 1939, and the First World War or World War I thereafter, was a major war centred in Europe that began on 28 July 1914 and lasted until 11 November 1918...

.

Early radio transmitters were incapable of handling the complexity of actual audio and therefore CW was the only form of communication available. CW still remained a viable form of radio communication for many years after voice transmission was perfected, because simple transmitters could be used. The low bandwidth of the code signal, due in part to low information transmission rate, allowed very selective filters to be used in the receiver which blocked out much of the atmospheric noise that would otherwise reduce the intelligibility of the signal.

On-off keying
On-off keying the simplest form of amplitude-shift keying modulation that represents digital data as the presence or absence<#sep#>You are tasked with analyzing user behavior on a website using a dataset that includes user interactions with timestamps and event types. Implement a function `analyze_user_behavior(data)` that processes this dataset and returns the following statistics:

1. The number of unique users.
2. The average time spent on the website per user in minutes.
3. The most common event type.
4. A list of the top 5 most visited pages.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents an interaction with the following keys:
  - `user_id`: An integer representing the user.
  - `timestamp`: A string representing the time of the interaction in the format 'YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS'.
  - `event_type`: A string representing the type of event (e.g., 'login', 'page_view', 'logout').
  - `page`: A string representing the page visited, or `None` if the event is not a page view.

**Output:**
- A tuple containing:
  1. An integer representing the number of unique users.
  2. A float representing the average time spent on the website per user in minutes.
  3. A string representing the most common event type.
  4. A list of strings representing the top 5 most visited pages.

**Sample Input:**
```python
data = [
    {'user_id': 1, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:00:00', 'event_type': 'login', 'page': None},
    {'user_id': 1, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:05:00', 'event_type': 'page_view', 'page': 'home.html'},
    {'user_id': 1, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:10:00', 'event_type': 'page_view', 'page': 'about.html'},
    {'user_id': 1, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:15:00', 'event_type': 'logout', 'page': None},
    {'user_id': 2, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:20:00', 'event_type': 'login', 'page': None},
    {'user_id': 2, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:25:00', 'event_type': 'page_view', 'page': 'home.html'},
    {'user_id': 2, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:30:00', 'event_type': 'page_view', 'page': 'services.html'},
    {'user_id': 2, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:35:00', 'event_type': 'page_view', 'page': 'home.html'},
    {'user_id': 2, 'timestamp': '2023-10-01 12:40:00', 'event_type': 'logout', 'page': None},
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
(2, 15.0, 'page_view', ['home.html', 'about.html', 'services.html'])
```

```python
import pandas as pd
from datetime import datetime
from collections import Counter

def analyze_user_behavior(data):
    """
    Analyzes user behavior on a website using a dataset of user interactions.

    Parameters:
    data (list of dict): A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents an interaction
                         with keys 'user_id', 'timestamp', 'event_type', and 'page'.

    Returns:
    tuple: A tuple containing:
           1. An integer representing the number of unique users.
           2. A float representing the average time spent on the website per user in minutes.
           3. A string representing the most common event type.
           4. A list of strings representing the top 5 most visited pages.
    """
    df = pd.DataFrame(data)
    df['timestamp'] = pd.to_datetime(df['timestamp'])
    df.sort_values(by=['user_id', 'timestamp'], inplace=True)
    df['time_diff'] = df.groupby('user_id')['timestamp'].diff().fillna(pd.Timedelta(0))
    df['time_diff_minutes'] = df['time_diff'].dt.total_seconds() / 60
    unique_users = df['user_id'].nunique()
    avg_time_spent = df.groupby('user_id')['time_diff_minutes'].sum().mean()
    most_common_event = df['event_type'].mode()[0]
    page_visits = df[df['event_type'] == 'page_view']['page'].value_counts().head(5)
    top_5_visited_pages = page_visits.index.tolist()
    return unique_users, avg_time_spent, most_common_event, top_5_visited_pages

+def heartbeat():
+    """Get the server specs"""
+    resp = None
+    if authorize(current_app.db, request):
+        LOG.info('Authorized client requests heartbeat..')
+        LOG.info('current_app is {}'.format(current_app))
+        disclaimer = current_app.config.get('DISCLAIMER')
+        result = controllers.heartbeat(disclaimer)
+        resp = jsonify(result)
+        resp.status_code = 200
+
+    else: # not authorized, return a 401 status code
+        return controllers.bad_request(401)
+
+    return resp
+

Ordinary crystal detectors cannot demodulate continuous waves; the direct current signal is inaudible in headphones...<#sep#>Implement a Python class `DatabaseConnection` that manages basic operations on an SQLite database, including connecting to the database, executing queries, and closing the connection. The class should handle exceptions gracefully. Your implementation should include methods for creating a connection, executing a query, fetching all rows from a query, fetching a single row from a query, and closing the connection.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
db = DatabaseConnection("example.db")
db.create_connection()
db.execute_query("CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT)")
db.execute_query("INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (?)", ("Alice",))
db.execute_query("INSERT INTO users (name) VALUES (?)", ("Bob",))
print(db.fetch_all("SELECT * FROM users"))
print(db.fetch_one("SELECT name FROM users WHERE id = ?", (1,)))
db.close_connection()
```

**Expected Output:**
```
Successfully connected to SQLite database: example.db
Query successfully executed
Query successfully executed
Query successfully executed
[(1, 'Alice'), (2, 'Bob')]
('Alice',)
Database connection closed
```

```python
import sqlite3
from sqlite3 import Error

class DatabaseConnection:
    def __init__(self, db_file):
        """
        Initialize the DatabaseConnection with the path to the database file.
        
        :param db_file: Path to the SQLite database file.
        """
        self.connection = None
        self.db_file = db_file

    def create_connection(self):
        """
        Create a database connection to the SQLite database.
        """
        try:
            self.connection = sqlite3.connect(self.db_file)
            print(f"Successfully connected to SQLite database: {self.db_file}")
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while connecting to the database: {e}")

    def execute_query(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Execute a single SQL query.
        
        :param query: SQL query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the query.
        :return: Cursor object if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        if self.connection is None:
            print("No database connection. Please call create_connection() first.")
            return None
        cursor = self.connection.cursor()
        try:
            if params:
                cursor.execute(query, params)
            else:
                cursor.execute(query)
            self.connection.commit()
            print("Query successfully executed")
            return cursor
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            cursor.close()

    def fetch_all(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Fetch all rows from the database using a SELECT query.
        
        :param query: SQL SELECT query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the query.
        :return: List of rows if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        cursor = self.execute_query(query, params)
        try:
            if cursor:
                rows = cursor.fetchall()
                return rows
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while fetching data: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            cursor.close()

    def fetch_one(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Fetch a single row from the database using a SELECT query.
        
        :param query: SQL SELECT query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the query.
        :return: Single row if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        cursor = self.execute_query(query, params)
        try:
            if cursor:
                row = cursor.fetchone()
                return row
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while fetching data: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            cursor.close()

    def close_connection(self):
        """
        Close the database connection.
        """
        try:
            if self.connection:
                self.connection.close()
                print("Database connection closed")
        except Error as e:
            print(f"An error occurred while closing the connection: {e}")

So maybe it's possible to have non-congruent triangles with the same circumradius. However, arranging five points in space such that every trio has a circumradius equal to some fixed R, and no four points are on a circle.

This seems complicated, but perhaps in 3D, there's a configuration. Alternatively, maybe n=4 is the maximum. Let's think of known configurations.

Another idea: if all the points lie on a sphere and every three points form a triangle with the same circumradius. Since the circumradius of a triangle on a sphere relates to the sphere's radius and the distance from the plane. As before, if all the planes are at the same distance from the center, then all circles have the same radius. So, if all points lie on a sphere and every trio of points lies on a plane at a fixed distance from the center, then all circles would be congruent.

Therefore, to have such a configuration, the points must lie on a sphere, and all 3-point planes must be tangent to another sphere (the one with radius d where d² + r² = R²). But how can we arrange points on a sphere such that every three of them lie on a plane tangent to a fixed inner sphere?

Wait, if the inner sphere is a point (radius zero), then all planes would have to pass through the center, meaning that all circles would be great circles. But that would require that every three points lie on a great circle. However, in that case, four points could lie on a great circle, violating the problem's condition. So that's not possible unless n ≤ 3. But even three points on a great circle: for example, three points on the equator of a sphere. Then any three points lie on the equator (a great circle), so all circles are congruent (radius equal to the sphere's radius). But if n=3, that's okay. For n=4, if we have four points on a sphere such that every three lie on a great circle, but no four on a great circle. But in reality, if four points are on a sphere, and every three lie on a great circle, then all four points must lie on the same great circle. Because if you have four points such that any three are on a great circle, then all four lie on that same circle. Hence, n=4 would require four cocircular points, violating the problem's condition. Therefore, arranging points on a sphere with all 3-point planes being great circles is impossible for n≥4.

Therefore, going back to the idea of planes tangent to an inner sphere. Suppose we have a sphere of radius R, and an inner sphere of radius d, such that all planes containing three points are tangent to the inner sphere. Then the circles would have radius r = √(R² - d²). Therefore, all circles would be congruent. Additionally, we need that no four points lie on a circle, which translates to no four points being coplanar. So each plane can contain at most three points. How can we arrange points on a sphere such that every three lie on a plane tangent to an inner sphere, and no four are coplanar?

This seems similar to the concept of a "spherical cap" configuration. Wait, but if all the planes are tangent to an inner sphere, then each plane touches the inner sphere at exactly one point. So, each trio of points lies on a plane that is tangent to the inner sphere, so the three points lie on a circle that is tangent to the inner sphere. This seems similar to the concept of a "Reuleaux tetrahedron," but I'm not sure.

Alternatively, think of the problem in terms of sphere inversion. Maybe not helpful.

Alternatively, think of the points as lying on the intersection of two spheres. But the intersection of two spheres is a circle. If all points lie on a circle, then any three points would lie on that circle, which would mean the circle has four points, which is not allowed. So that's not helpful.

Another thought: if all circles have the same radius, then all triangles formed by three points must have the same circumradius. So maybe all triangles are congruent? But in 3D, you can have triangles with the same circumradius but different shapes. However, maybe in such a configuration, all triangles would need to be congruent. For example, in a regular tetrahedron, all faces are congruent equilateral triangles. Therefore, adding another point would require that all new triangles formed with this point are congruent to the existing ones, which might not be possible.

Alternatively, perhaps the configuration is a regular polyhedron. But as we saw with the octahedron, even though all triangles are congruent, they allow four points to lie on a circle. So regular polyhedrons beyond the tetrahedron might not work. The cube, for instance: any three vertices may form different triangles. For example, a face diagonal and edges. Wait, the regular tetrahedron might be the only regular polyhedron that satisfies the conditions. Since in the tetrahedron, any three points form a face, and all faces are congruent, with no four points on a circle. So n=4 is possible. Then, can we have n=5?

Alternatively, consider configurations in 3D where points are arranged such that every three form a congruent circle. Maybe a pyramid with a regular base. For example, a square pyramid. If the base is a square and the apex is directly above the center. Let's see: take the base vertices as (1,1,0), (-1,1,0), (-1,-1,0), (1,-1,0), and apex at (0,0,h). Then, take three points: two from the base and the apex. The circumradius of that triangle would depend on h. If we set h such that the circumradius is equal to the circumradius of the base triangles. The base triangles (each face of the square) are right triangles with legs 2 and 2, hypotenuse 2√2. The circumradius of a right triangle is half the hypotenuse, so √2. For the triangle formed by the apex and two adjacent base vertices: the triangle has sides √(h² + 2²), √(h² + 2²), and 2√2. The circumradius R of this triangle is given by R = (a b c)/(4 Δ), where Δ is the area.

Let's compute for h. Let's take the triangle with vertices (1,1,0), (-1,1,0), (0,0,h). The sides:

Between (1,1,0) and (-1,1,0): 2√1² +0 +0 = 2.

Between (1,1,0) and (0,0,h): √(1² +1² + h²) = √(2 + h²).

Similarly for (-1,1,0) to (0,0,h): √(2 + h²).

So triangle sides: 2, √(2 + h²), √(2 + h²). This is an isoceles triangle.

The area Δ can be calculated as (base * height)/2. Take the base as 2. The height can be found from coordinates. Alternatively, use Heron's formula.

s = (2 + √(2 + h²) + √(2 + h²))/2 = 1 + √(2 + h²).

Area = √[s(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)].

But maybe easier to compute using coordinates. The vectors from (0,0,h) to (1,1,0) and (-1,1,0) are (1,1,-h) and (-1,1,-h). The cross product of these vectors is:

|i     j     k|
|1     1    -h|
|-1    1    -h|

= i*(1*(-h) - (-h)*1) - j*(1*(-h) - (-h)*(-1)) + k*(1*1 - (-1)*1)

= i*(-h + h) - j*(-h - h) + k*(1 +1)

= 0i + 2h j + 2k

So the magnitude of the cross product is √(0² + (2h)^2 + 2²) = √(4h² +4) = 2√(h² +1). Therefore, the area is half that, which is √(h² +1).

Re: Long Debrief - 800 (Q51, V51, IR-8, AWA-4.5) [#permalink<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Polynomial` class in Python that represents a polynomial in one variable. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Addition** (`+`): Add two polynomials.
2. **Subtraction** (`-`): Subtract one polynomial from another.
3. **Multiplication** (`*`): Multiply two polynomials.
4. **Division** (`/`): Divide one polynomial by another, returning both the quotient and remainder.
5. **Evaluation**: Evaluate the polynomial at a given point.
6. **Root Finding**: Find the roots of the polynomial.
7. **String Representation**: Print the polynomial in a standard form.

Your class should be able to handle polynomials of arbitrary degree. Implement a test script to demonstrate the functionality of your class.

**Sample Input/Output:**

For the polynomials \( P(x) = 1 + 2x + 3x^2 \) and \( Q(x) = 1 + 3x \):

- **Addition**: \( P(x) + Q(x) = 2 + 5x + 3x^2 \)
- **Subtraction**: \( P(x) - Q(x) = 3x^2 - x \)
- **Multiplication**: \( P(x) \times Q(x) = 1 + 5x + 12x^2 + 9x^3 \)
- **Division**: \( P(x) \div Q(x) \) yields quotient \( 3x - 1 \) and remainder \( 4 \)
- **Evaluation at \( x = 2 \)**: \( P(2) = 17 \), \( Q(2) = 7 \)
- **Roots**: Roots of \( P(x) \) are approximately \(-0.3473\) and \(-0.9527\), roots of \( Q(x) \) are approximately \(-0.3333\)

```python
import numpy as np
from numpy.polynomial import Polynomial as P

class Polynomial:
    """
    Represents a polynomial in one variable.
    """
    
    def __init__(self, coefficients):
        """
        Initialize a polynomial with a list of coefficients.
        Coefficient list is in the form [a0, a1, a2, ..., an] where
        a0 + a1*x + a2*x^2 + ... + an*x^n.
        """
        self.coefficients = np.array(coefficients)

    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Add two polynomials.
        """
        return Polynomial(np.polyadd(self.coefficients, other.coefficients))

    def __sub__(self, other):
        """
        Subtract two polynomials.
        """
        return Polynomial(np.polysub(self.coefficients, other.coefficients))

    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Multiply two polynomials.
        """
        return Polynomial(np.polymul(self.coefficients, other.coefficients))
    
    def __truediv__(self, other):
        """
        Divide two polynomials, return quotient and remainder.
        """
        quot, rem = np.polydiv(self.coefficients, other.coefficients)
        return Polynomial(list(map(np.rint, quot))), Polynomial(list(map(np.rint, rem)))

    def evaluate(self, x):
        """
        Evaluate the polynomial at a given point x.
        """
        return np.polyval(self.coefficients, x)

    def find_roots(self):
        """
        Find the roots of the polynomial.
        """
        return P(self.coefficients).roots()

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Print the polynomial in a standard form.
        """
        terms = []
        for power, coeff in enumerate(self.coefficients):
            if power == 0:
                terms.append(f"{coeff:.0f}")
            elif power == 1:
                terms.append(f"{coeff:.0f}x")
            else:
                terms.append(f"{coeff:.0f}x^{power}")
        
        terms = [term for term in reversed(terms) if not term.startswith("0")]
        
        if not terms:
            return "0"
        elif len(terms) == 1:
            return terms[0]
        else:
            return " + ".join(terms).replace("+ -", "- ")

# Test script
if __name__ == "__main__":
    poly1 = Polynomial([1, 2, 3])  # Represents 1 + 2x + 3x^2
    poly2 = Polynomial([1, 3])     # Represents 1 + 3x
    
    # Print polynomials
    print(f"Polynomial 1: {poly1}")
    print(f"Polynomial 2: {poly2}")

    # Evaluate polynomials at x = 2
    print(f"Evaluation of Polynomial 1 at x = 2: {poly1.evaluate(2)}")
    print(f"Evaluation of Polynomial 2 at x = 2: {poly2.evaluate(2)}")

    # Find roots of polynomials
    print(f"Roots of Polynomial 1: {poly1.find_roots()}")
    print(f"Roots of Polynomial 2: {poly2.find_roots()}")

    # Perform arithmetic operations
    add_result = poly1 + poly2
    sub_result = poly1 - poly2
    mul_result = poly1 * poly2
    div_quot, div_rem = poly1 / poly2

    print(f"Adding: {add_result}")
    print(f"Subtracting: {sub_result}")
    print(f"Multiplying: {mul_result}")
    print(f"Dividing: Quotient {div_quot}, Remainder {div_rem}")

NonYankee wrote:
Update 3 (Aug 11, 2012): I have received multiple requests for the study documents in which I compiled Bunuel's quant problems. I have (hopefully) removed all personally identifying information from the files and attached them as a zip file below. Good luck, everyone!
Update 2: I just got my official scores. My IR score was 8 (despite not answering the final two questions) and I basically bombed the essay... AWA was 4.5. I can't really imagine what caused my essay to be so poor. I know that waiting for 30 minutes before the test made me nervous. Or maybe I just wrote the wrong stuff!
Update: I want to thank everyone for their kind words and support. In case anyone is interested (or incredulous), I've added a Notice of GMAT Testing Irregularities file that I received from the Pearson Vue Security Team. It simply says I'm not allowed to take the GMAT for five years. I'm under the impression that this is standard procedure.

Hello everyone,

I haven't posted very much on GMAT Club forums before now because I never thought I had anything worthwhile to share, but now that I got a decent good GMAT score, maybe my debrief will help someone.

My Background

I am a 25 year-old American male. I have always performed above average on standardized tests. I had great success with the ACT especially. I didn't care as much for the SAT. I remember the obscure vocabulary and analogies to be quite challenging.

I received a BS in chemical engineering and a MS in bioengineering from a good-but-not-great state school (although our football team is top-notch). After my master's I joined the Peace Corps (in 2010). My work mostly involves community development and English education in Ukraine.

Method of Study and GMAT Score History

I used the OG 12 and GMATPrep software. I liked the OG because it provides answer explanations. Because of the introduction of the IR section, I was actually able to use two versions of GMATPrep software (before and after the change). This allowed me to have 4 adaptive practice exams. In general, I never studied on two consecutive days, nor for longer than 3 hours at a time. When using the OG, I enjoyed working through one question type at my own pace without a time limit.

First GMATPrep Practice Exam

Last summer (2011) I determined that I wanted to pursue an MBA after Peace Corps if I would be accepted to a highly ranked program. I downloaded the GMATPrep software from mba.com. I took a practice exam on July 13, 2011. I received a score above 700. I don't remember what it was exactly, but I remember thinking that while the score wasn't amazing, it wasn't so low that I felt nervous about preparing for the test. I forgot about the GMAT for a while.

Registering for the Test and Practicing Diagnostic Questions

On April 29, 2012 I finally registered to take the GMAT in Kyiv, Ukraine on July 3, 2012. I wanted to take it before it changed in early June, but I didn't want to miss work at the end of the school year (since I'm a teacher) and the dates just didn't work out. I had the good fortune of finding a copy of GMAC's Official Guide, 12th Edition among the free books that volunteers leave in the Peace Corps office. This guide has a short diagnostic test (paper-based) that I used to test myself. I intended to answer the quantitative questions, find how many I'd missed, then answer the verbal questions, but I never got to the verbal questions. I was appalled that I had missed about 10% of the quantitative diagnostic questions. I reviewed my mistakes and realized that half of them were due to careless mental math errors (saying 12 divided by 1.5 = 6, or 4 out of 20 is 25%) and the other half were due to reading the question incorrectly, thereby missing important details. I didn't do any other studying in April.

Working with the Official Guide (12th Ed.)

Subsequent GMATPrep Practice Exams

Still using the old GMATPrep software (based on Java), I took my second practice exam on June 26, 2012. For the Analysis of an Argument section, I simply typed out an outline with sections such as: argument's thesis, supporting evidence, underlying assumptions, alternative explanations, and other information that would be useful. I didn't write an actual essay. I decided to skip the Analysis of an Issue section, since it wouldn't be on my actual exam (I would take the Next Generation GMAT with Integrated Reasoning). I finished the quantitative and verbal sections in good time. It was only later that I realized how much<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `FileHandler` that provides a simple interface for file operations. Your class should support the following functionalities:

1. **Read File**: Implement a method `read_file(file_path)` that reads the contents of a file and returns it as a string. If the file does not exist, raise a `FileNotFoundError`.

2. **Write File**: Implement a method `write_file(file_path, content)` that writes a string to a file, overwriting the file if it already exists.

3. **Append to File**: Implement a method `append_to_file(file_path, content)` that appends a string to the end of a file. If the file does not exist, it should be created.

4. **Delete File**: Implement a method `delete_file(file_path)` that deletes a file. If the file does not exist, raise a `FileNotFoundError`.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- `read_file("example.txt")` should return the contents of "example.txt" if it exists.
- `write_file("example.txt", "Hello, World!")` should create or overwrite "example.txt" with "Hello, World!".
- `append_to_file("example.txt", " Goodbye!")` should append " Goodbye!" to "example.txt".
- `delete_file("example.txt")` should delete "example.txt" if it exists.

```python
import os

class FileHandler:
    
    def read_file(self, file_path):
        """
        Read the contents of a file and return it as a string.
        
        :param file_path: Path to the file to read.
        :return: The contents of the file as a string.
        :raises FileNotFoundError: If the file does not exist.
        """
        try:
            with open(file_path, 'r') as file:
                return file.read()
        except FileNotFoundError:
            raise FileNotFoundError(f"The file at {file_path} does not exist.")
    
    def write_file(self, file_path, content):
        """
        Write a string to a file, overwriting the file if it already exists.
        
        :param file_path: Path to the file to write to.
        :param content: The string to write to the file.
        """
        with open(file_path, 'w') as file:
            file.write(content)
    
    def append_to_file(self, file_path, content):
        """
        Append a string to the end of a file, creating the file if it does not exist.
        
        :param file_path: Path to the file to append to.
        :param content: The string to append to the file.
        """
        with open(file_path, 'a') as file:
            file.write(content)
    
    def delete_file(self, file_path):
        """
        Delete a file. Raise FileNotFoundError if the file does not exist.
        
        :param file_path: Path to the file to delete.
        :raises FileNotFoundError: If the file does not exist.
        """
        try:
            os.remove(file_path)
        except FileNotFoundError:
            raise FileNotFoundError(f"The file at {file_path} does not exist.")
```<#sep#> it's impossible. Hence, the possible n are 3 and 4.

Wait, but the problem says "find all integers n ≥3". So if both 3 and 4 are possible, but 5 and above aren't, then the answer is n=3 and n=4.

But wait, the problem may be only possible for n=3 and n=4. But I need to verify if n=5 is indeed impossible.

Another approach: Assume that such a configuration exists for n ≥5. Then, each pair of points must lie on multiple circles (each circle defined by a third point). But each pair can be part of multiple circles. However, in such a configuration, there must be constraints on the distances between points. If all circles are congruent, then for any three points, the triangle formed has the same circumradius. This would impose that all triangles have the same circumradius. Is there a way to arrange five or more points in 3D space so that every triangle has the same circumradius?

In 2D, if all triangles have the same circumradius, then all points lie on a circle (the circumcircle), but the problem prohibits four points on a circle. But in 3D, it's possible to have points on a sphere with each triangle having the same circumradius, but as before, arranging them so that no four are coplanar.

However, even on a sphere, if you require that every three points lie on a small circle (not a great circle) of the same radius, this is equivalent to all planes being at the same distance from the center. But as we discussed earlier, this requires that all planes are tangent to a common inner sphere.

But how many points can lie on a sphere such that every three are on a plane tangent to an inner sphere? This is similar to the concept of a "spherical design," but more specific.

In such a case, each point must lie on multiple tangent planes. For example, if we have an inner sphere of radius d, and the outer sphere of radius R, with d² + r² = R² where r is the radius of the circles. Each plane tangent to the inner sphere cuts the outer sphere in a circle of radius r. Each such plane can contain multiple points, but the problem states no four points on a circle, so each plane can contain at most three points.

Therefore, each point must be on several such planes, each containing two other points. The question is: how many points can be arranged on the sphere so that every trio is on a unique tangent plane, and no four points are on a single tangent plane.

This seems related to combinatorial designs, specifically Steiner systems. A Steiner triple system S(t, k, v) where t=3, k=3, v=n. But in our case, each triple is in exactly one block (plane), and each pair is in exactly λ blocks. However, in 3D space, each pair of points can lie in multiple planes (each with a different third point). Therefore, it's not exactly a Steiner system. But perhaps a different combinatorial structure.

Alternatively, consider that each pair of points lies on multiple tangent planes, each corresponding to a third point. For n points, the number of triples is C(n,3), and each tangent plane contains exactly one triple. But each tangent plane is determined by the three points it contains, and since the planes are all tangent to the inner sphere, they must each touch the inner sphere at a unique point. Therefore, the number of tangent planes needed is C(n,3). However, the number of distinct tangent planes possible on a sphere is infinite, but we are limited by the requirement that each plane contains exactly three points.

However, the problem is with the geometry: how to arrange n points on a sphere such that every three lie on a plane tangent to an inner sphere, and no four lie on any such plane. Such a configuration would require that the inner sphere is touched by C(n,3) distinct planes, each containing exactly three points of the configuration. This seems possible only for small n.

In the case of n=4 (tetrahedron), the four points on the outer sphere would have C(4,3)=4 tangent planes. Each face of the tetrahedron is a plane tangent to the inner sphere. So for a regular tetrahedron, the inner sphere would be the insphere, tangent to each face. Wait, in a regular tetrahedron, the insphere is tangent to each face, yes. The inradius is the distance from the center to each face, which we computed earlier as a/(2√6). So if the regular tetrahedron's circumradius is R = a√6/4, then the inradius r = a√6/12. Then, the radius of each circle (the intersection of the face plane with the outer sphere) would be √(R² - r²). Wait, but R is the circumradius of the tetrahedron, which is the distance from center to vertices. The inradius is the distance from center to the faces. The radius of the circle from the face plane would be the circumradius of the face triangle, which is a√3/3. Let's check: if R = a√6/4, then the face plane's distance from center is d = inradius = a√6/12. Then, the radius of the circle is √(R² - d²) = √( (6a²/16) - (6a²/144) ) = √( (54a²/144 - 6a²/144) ) = √(48a²/144) = √(a²/3) = a/√3. Which is indeed the circumradius of the face triangle (which has edge length a). Therefore, in the regular tetrahedron case, yes, each face is a plane tangent to the insphere, and the circles have radius a/√3, all congruent.

So for n=4, this works. Now, can we have n=5? To do this, we would need five points on the outer sphere, such that every three lie on a plane tangent to the inner sphere, and no four on a plane. This would require that there are C(5,3)=10 tangent planes, each containing exactly three points, and no four points lying on any of these planes. However, arranging five points on a sphere with this property seems non-trivial. In fact, it might be impossible due to geometric constraints.

In combinatorial terms, this is similar to having a 3-design where each triple is a block, but in geometry, the blocks are planes. However, spherical 3-designs require that points are arranged so that certain averages are preserved, which is a different concept.

Another approach: use duality. The dual of the outer sphere with points and tangent planes is equivalent to points and planes in dual space, but I don't think that helps.

Alternatively, think of the problem in terms of the Miquel theorem or other circle theorems, but in 3D.

Alternatively, consider that the problem is only possible for n=3 and n=4. Since in 3D, the regular tetrahedron is the only configuration where every three points form a congruent circle, and adding more points disrupts this. Therefore, the answer is n=3 and n=4.

However, the problem says "Find all integers n ≥ 3". So, perhaps the possible n are 3 and 4. But I need to confirm if n=4 is indeed the maximum.

In conclusion, after considering various configurations and the constraints that no four points are cocircular and all circles through three points are congruent, the regular tetrahedron (n=4) satisfies the conditions, and n=3 trivially does. Higher n seem impossible due to the requirement of either forcing four points on a circle or having non-congruent circles.

**Final Answer**
The integers are \boxed{3} and \boxed{4}.

   - No four points are cocircular.
   - All circles passing through any three points must be congruent, meaning they have the same radius.

   - Three non-collinear points form a single circle.
   - Since there are no four points, the cocircular condition is vacuously true.
   - Therefore, \( n = 3 \) satisfies all conditions.

**⚠️ Important:** Please avoid sharing any sensitive or personal information, including text or images, in this issue.

**Is your feature request related to a problem in the current program to new available techology or software? Please describe and add links/citations if appropriate.**

In the next release of balsamic we're planning to enable soft-filtering of SNVs present in the matched normal (the current matched normal filters will be unchanged but will not be hard-filtered), and as a result variants will be uploaded to Scout with a couple of new filters. See issue in MTP-BALSAMIC: https://github.com/Clinical-Genomics/MTP-BALSAMIC/issues/1

At the moment we have 2 filters which are both used to filter out variants based on presence in the normal. There is "germline_risk" from TNscope, and a custom bcftools filter which used to be called "high_normal_tumor_af_frac" but which we will rename in balsamic v17.0.0 to "in_normal". 

![Image](https://github.com/user-attachments/assets/f9da42b2-5336-4c82-803b-04f856548820)

This was requested by some customers mainly due to the risk of filtering out clinically relevant variants with a high presence in the normal, but will cause some institutes in Scout to receive variants they are not used to seeing, and which they likely do not wish to see, and to avoid this it would be nice to have easy option to filtering out these "high normal" variants by default for these institutes by applying the balsamic soft-filters.

**Describe the solution you'd like**

We discussed adding another checkbox here:

![Image](https://github.com/user-attachments/assets/070b8274-e281-4950-a809-d3c0e9d68c8e)

Which can be set to default removing the soft filtered normal variants on an institute level. But I trust in the scout-devs to figure out the best solution! 🙏 

**Describe alternatives you've considered**
A clear and concise description of any alternative solutions or features you've considered.

**Additional context**

This feature is planned to be activated on the balsamic side in release 17.0.0, for all T+N matched panel analyses, which will increase the number of variants uploaded to Scout for this analysis type. But as the common germline variants are mostly filtered out by population databases the increase shouldn't be that massive. They will in essence in terms of number of variants become like tumor only analyses, which based on what I have seen would roughly increase the number of variants from 2k to 4k, but it varies on the panel.

To get a sense of what this will mean for the total number of variants uploaded from balsamic I counted the current number of tumor only cases in the production directory to 187, and the number of tumor normal cases to 19. Which means that roughly for every 10 tumor only case there's a tumor normal. As a very rough guesstimate the total number of variants from balsamic should with this feature increase by roughly 5%. 

But together with new filters planned to be added in 17.0.0 probably the net variants will actually decrease. It could also be looked into how this feature could be selectively implemented for certain institutes / or optionally per order.

The class should have the following attributes: `title`, `author`, `year_of_publication`, `genre`, and `ISBN`. The `ISBN` should be a randomly generated 13-digit number and should be read-only. The `year_of_publication` should not exceed the current year. Implement methods to update the `title` and `genre`, and a method to display the book's information in a formatted string.

**Sample Input:**
```
"1984", "George Orwell", 1949, "Dystopian"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Title: 1984
Author: George Orwell
Year of Publication: 1949
Genre: Dystopian
ISBN: 1234567890123  # Example ISBN, actual output will vary
```

```python
import random
from datetime import datetime

class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year_of_publication, genre):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given title, author, year of publication, and genre.
        The ISBN is generated randomly and is read-only.
        """
        self._title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year_of_publication = year_of_publication
        self._genre = genre
        self._ISBN = self._generate_isbn()

    @property
    def title(self):
        """
        Returns the title of the book.
        """
        return self._title

    @title.setter
    def title(self, new_title):
        """
        Sets a new title for the book.
        """
        self._title = new_title

    @property
    def genre(self):
        """
        Returns the genre of the book.
        """
        return self._genre

    @genre.setter
    def genre(self, new_genre):
        """
        Sets a new genre for the book.
        """
        self._genre = new_genre

    @property
    def ISBN(self):
        """
        Returns the ISBN of the book. This attribute is read-only.
        """
        return self._ISBN

    @property
    def year_of_publication(self):
        """
        Returns the year of publication of the book.
        """
        return self._year_of_publication

    @year_of_publication.setter
    def year_of_publication(self, value):
        """
        Sets the year of publication for the book, ensuring it does not exceed the current year.
        """
        current_year = datetime.now().year
        if value > current_year:
            raise ValueError("Year of publication cannot be greater than the current year.")
        self._year_of_publication = value

    def _generate_isbn(self):
        """
        Generates a random 13-digit ISBN number.
        """
        return ''.join(str(random.randint(0, 9)) for _ in range(13))

    def display_info(self):
        """
        Returns a formatted string containing the book's information.
        """
        return (f"Title: {self.title}\n"
                f"Author: {self.author}\n"
                f"Year of Publication: {self.year_of_publication}\n"
                f"Genre: {self.genre}\n"
                f"ISBN: {self.ISBN}")
```<#sep#> No three points are collinear.
   - No four points are cocircular.
   - All circles passing through any three points must be congruent, meaning they have the same radius.

2. **Case \( n = 3 \)**:
   - Three non-collinear points form a single circle.
   - Since there are no four points, the cocircular condition is vacuously true.
   - Therefore, \( n = 3 \) satisfies all conditions.

3. **Case \( n = 4 \)**:
   - Consider a regular tetrahedron in 3D space.
   - Each face of the tetrahedron is an equilateral triangle.
   - The circumradius of each face is the same.
   - No four points are coplanar, hence no four points are cocircular.
   - All circles passing through any three points (faces of the tetrahedron) are congruent.
   - Therefore, \( n = 4 \) satisfies all conditions.

4. **Higher \( n \ge 5 \)**:
   - We need to check if it is possible to arrange five or more points such that all circles passing through any three points are congruent and no four points are cocircular.
   - Consider a regular polyhedron with more than four vertices (e.g., octahedron).
   - In an octahedron, four vertices lie on a circle, violating the cocircular condition.
   - Other configurations involving five or more points generally fail to maintain congruent circles or result in four points being cocircular.
   - Therefore, higher \( n \ge 5 \) configurations are impossible due to geometric constraints.

### Conclusion:
The only integers \( n \ge 3 \) that satisfy all the given conditions are \( n = 3 \) and \( n = 4 \).

### Final Answer:
The integers are \(\boxed{3}\) and \(\boxed{4}\).<#sep#> the number of delta edges which can be chosen as part of the basis.

Hence the count should be 2^ (number of independent basis vectors not affecting the minimal's bits) ).

Hence, my previous thoughts were not correct.

 To see this, suppose that the minimal XOR is not affected by certain vectors in the basis. 

 For example, suppose one basis vector has bits only in the lower positions which are not affecting the minimal XOR's prioritized bits.

Then you can choose to toggle it or not, leading to different spanning trees with the same minimal XOR.

Ah! Here is the key point:

To have the minimal XOR value, certain basis vectors must be taken (or not taken) in the selection to form the delta. 

The degrees of freedom are over the basis vectors that do not affect the minimal's value.

So the idea is:

 The minimal is computed by selecting certain basis vectors. The unused basis vectors (those whose bit patterns do not influence whether the XOR is minimal) can be chosen freely, hence each such basis contributes a multiplicative factor.

 The number of ways is 2^ (number of basis vectors that are independent and not affecting the minimal value). 

故， the total count is^- equal to 2^( number of basis vectors that are not used in the minimal’s choice? 

因此， the algorithm is:

- Compute the minimal by applying the basis vectors from highest bit down, to choose which are needed to set to minimum the value. 

The basis vectors which are used can have no other options, but those which are not used in the path can be chosen freely or not.

 Thus, the number of ways is 2^(number of basis vectors not involved in this process).

 准确来说， the basis vectors are processed in order of highest bit to lowest. For each vector, if it affects the current highest bit, it must be chosen. Once the minimal is computed, all other vectors are still part of the basis， but were not necessarily contributing to the minimal. 

 Thus, if there are vector basis that can be chosen in both ways without changing the final result, the count is multiplied by 2 for each of them. 

Thus, the number is the product of the possible choices for the basis vectors that are independent and do not affect the final minimal value.

 Hence, the count is 2^\ the number of basis vectors that are not required to be chosen in the minimal process). 

 How many are they?

 During the minimal's computation, when processing basis vectors in order of highest down, some may have been chosen (Xor'd) to the final vector, because their leading bit was set in the current value.

 The basis vectors not chosen contribute to the degrees of freedom. 

Thus, the number of free basis vectors is the basis size minus the number of basis vectors chosen to flip their leading bit. 

 Wait, perhaps the correct way is that the basis vectors that are not part of any of the decision-making steps can be chosen freely because they don't affect the minimal's bits. 

 More precisely:

 For each basis vector b in basis： if bits higher than 其 leading bit are not set in the target minimal, then they can be toggled in any way. 

 Thus, the degrees of freedom are the number of basis vectors with leading bits that are unpicked in the minimal selection.

 Hence, during the minimal computation:

 Basis vectors are sorted by leading bit (highest to lowest).

 The current value starts as the initial S.

 For each basis vector in this sorted list:

     if ( the current value has the leading bit of this basis vector set ):

             we nust XOR it with the basis vector, because this bit is the highest possible, and flipping it would bring the value lower.

     Thus， this vector can't contribute to the choices; it must be obtained in the process of deciding the minimal.

     so the basis vector is in the set to XOR.

     the current value ^= b.

 At the end, all the basis vectors whose leading bits were set are required to be toggled (to minimize their contribution), while the others can be chosen however ( 他们的 toggling has no effect on the current value's being already minimized in higher bits).

 Thus， the free basis vectors are those which leading bits are not required to be toggled, because their leading bit was not in the path of the minimal.

 These free vectors can be XORed or not independently, and thus contribute to the count as 2^(number of such vectors).

 Thus the count is 2^{ the number of basis vectors not used in the minimal construction}).

 How many such vectors are there?

 The total basis _ size is rank.

 The number of basis vectors that were used is the count of basis vectors where the leading bit v was such that the current value during processing had that bit set.

 Thus, the count of free basis vectors is (rank - used#{used}).

 Thus,, the final count is 2 power (rank - used).

 Thus, this is the way to calculate the number of ways.

 Aha! That makes sense now.]

 Thus， the algorithm would:

 - Find the basis vector's basis. 

 - Process them in order of their leading bit (descending).

 - Count how many basis vectors are chosen during the minimal computation (used).

 - The number of ways is 2^{ (basis size) - (used)} }.

 Thus is the way to compute the count.

 This is the correct approach.

Thus, the steps are:

1. Find the initial spanning tree.

2. Compute all deltas for non-tree edges.

3. Compute the basis of these deltas (call this basis).

3. Sort the basis vectors by leadbit from high to low.

4. Compute the initial S (x0= initial_xor):

5. For each basis vector in turn (from highest to lowest lead bit):

   a.if the current x0's lead bit equals this basis’ lead bit:

        → XOR with it, increment used, and proceed.

        → the current x0 ^ by basis vector.

6. At the end the"x_min" is x0 after this process.

 Thus, the minimal XOR is x0, and the number of ways is 2^( number basis大小 - used).

 So the count is the number of basis vectors not in the used set.

 This makes sense.

 Thus the example:

 The basis vectors: in their basis after processing have perhaps some rank, and certain are used.

 The total rank is the number of basis vectors made.

 Thus the count is 2^( basis.size - used).

 Thus, the example would have basis vectors count = used, hence count is 1.

So the algorithm is correct.

 Now, the challenge is to implement this.

 First step: build the initial spanning tree.

 To build the spanning tree, we can use BFS.

 The capital is perhaps node 1.

 To represent the parents for the tree.

 We will need for each non-tree edge to find the path between its endpoints to compute the delta.

 To compute the path's XOR, we can precompute for each node, the path back to the root's XOR path.

 Thus, when we have a node u and v, the path between their parents would follow their paths to root.

 However, computing the XOR path between two nodes in a tree can be done via a path finding method. 

 这 is a problem.

Handling this with binary lifting or another method for LCA and path queries.

 But with N up to 1e5~1e5 nodes, this is computationally feasible, but perhaps we can use a stack approach for XOR path computations.

 Alternatively, using BFS/DFS to compute ancestor's information.

 Here's a more efficient way:

 We can choose the root (capital) as node 1.

 For each node, we will record the XOR of the path from the root to that node. 

 Then, the path between any two nodes u and v can be computed as XOR of path to root from u and path to root from v, but considering the LCA.

However, the XOR path between u and v would be computed as the path from u to root and then root to v, but since XOR is associative and commutative, the path’s XOR is xor_path[u] ^ xor_path[v].
