+      {% if institute.soft_filters %} <!--<#sep#> those and be fine. In the end, I didn't have time to answer the final two questions I don't know my IR score yet, but I'm hopeful that I'll get a 7 or 8, or that my IR score won't count for too much, since business schools will still be adapting to the new GMAT format.

I took the first optional break. According to the lock screen on the computer, I had 8 minutes, but Vladislav told me I would have only 5 minutes. I went to the hallway, drank some juice, and cursed myself for running out of time on IR. I tried to calm myself before returning from my break, but it was no use.

The quantitative section went smoothly, except that one of the pens supplied to me didn't write very well. It would make two lines whenever I made a stroke. I read each question, then solved it on my laminated 10-sheet notepad, then selected my answer, then checked to see that the answer worked. I spent the most time on the DS questions and on PS questions that were structured with three conditions (I, II, III) and answer choices like (I only, II only, I and III, etc.). I assume most GMAT Club members know what I'm talking about. Even though I checked each question after selecting my answer, I finished the section in good time. I think I had 6-8 minutes left. I was relieved that I didn't see any complex permutation questions like those I'd stressed about in Bunuel's problem sets.

I took the second optional break. I sipped juice. I used the bathroom. I rinsed my face with water. I fussed about my appearance in the mirror. I thought about my performance on the quantitative section and felt that there was still hope for me to get a good overall score (this is in contrast to how I felt after IR). It all depended on the verbal section.

I've always found SC relatively easy. Verbs should agree with nouns, phrases should logically describe their noun (and that noun should be the closest noun to the phrase in almost every case), and there are a few finer points (like the differences between "fewer" and "less," "between" and "among") that aren't difficult to remember. On RC questions I took care to identify the basic content of each paragraph, and from the content I deduced the purpose of the paragraph. I also payed attention to the language used throughout the passage, which can sometimes make the purpose of the passage clear. On CR questions it's most important to identify which answer choices have nothing to do with the topic at hand. I reached the last verbal question, a SC-type, with about 6 minutes left. It was at this point that I realized I hadn't looked at the timer throughout the entire verbal section. Thinking back, I only glanced at the timer two or three times during the quantitative section. I realized that over the course of the test, I had become a kind of question-answering zombie--I had no awareness of anything besides the present question. I didn't even recall the question immediately prior to the current one. As the exam had worn on, I came to be entirely focused on the current prompt, what it was really asking, and which answer choices were viable and which were nonsense. I took my time and ended my exam with about 3-4 minutes remaining.

I paused when I saw the report/cancel scores screen. It was the first time I'd seen it, since it wasn't present in the GMATPrep software. I felt good about my test performance with the exception of IR. Then the unofficial score screen came up:

Quantitative: 51 Percentile: 98
Verbal: 51 Percentile: 99
Total: 800 Percentile: 99

I got goosebumps when I saw it! I raised my hand to be dismissed and shakily walked with Vladislav to his station, where he printed out my unofficial score report and scanned my palm one last time. He said it was the first time in two years that he'd seen a perfect score.

I can't say I'm a GMAT expert. But I highly recommend all GMAT examinees get acquainted with both the content and the length of the test. I was content with the official guide and GMATPrep software, both of which were produced by GMAC. For specific advanced questions, GMAT Club met all my needs.

Mental fatigue is real and it tends to snowball. I mean that as you approach the end of a section, fatigue will cause you to think more slowly, it will force you to re-read or re-calculate, and spending longer on each question will only serve to fatigue you even more. In the weeks leading up to your exam, take a few practice exams. I recommend taking them at the same time of day you as your test appointment. If possible, determine whether you are mentally fresher in the morning or afternoon before you schedule your exam.

Also, time management is important.

To those expecting scores of 700+, I would say do not pay too much attention to the clock. You have the ability to identify the correct answer, you just need to do it without second-guessing yourself or getting distracted. It might seem counter-intuitive, but I really believe my time management was better because I wasn't paying attention to the time. Don't calculate how much time per question you have remaining. Don't determine whether you are ahead or behind schedule. Focus instead on identifying what the question is asking and which answer choices are decoys or traps. It's important to avoid diverting your attention from the question at hand and losing your train of thought (on the current question) and your momentum (on the test section).

For those who aren't merely struggling against time, I would advise you to use strategies to eliminate answer choices whenever possible. This way, even if you truly don't know how to solve a question, your guesses will be correct more often. That's the goal: to narrow the choices and to do it as quickly as possible. On quantitative questions, use estimates when possible. On questions such as:

Which of the following must be true?
I. x<y<z
II. y<z<x
III. z<x<y

use test (example) numbers when possible, especially testing values between 0 and 1, between 1 and 2, and between -2 and -1 and -1 and 0. Don't forget to test 0 itself! And on SC questions, identify the noun and verb and ignore the answer choices that don't have the right form. On RC questions, an answer choice might use words from the passage but combine them to make a statement that is inconsistent with the content of the passage. Don't be deceived by the presence of conspicuous keywords from the passage. I think the strategies I've listed here will help eliminate the most incorrect answer choices the fastest, thereby helping you finish the section in time. Finally, pay attention on all questions to what's NOT said. Don't add or imagine that an opinion was stated in a passage or a constraint was listed in a quantitative prompt, when in fact there was no such thing.

Conclusion

I'm glad to put the GMAT behind me for now. The initial excitement of my score has worn off and now everything is firmly back in perspective. After all, if a school's student body has a median GMAT score of 700, it means that half of the accepted students scored 700 or below. That means that they were accepted because of other deciding factors and the GMAT didn't change the strength of their application. Similarly, I know that my GMAT won't get me into any schools that don't already like my essays, interview, and work experience.

I don't claim that luck played no part. I know that I didn't get every quantitative question right; otherwise I would have gotten a quantitative score of 60 according to GMAC's percentiles. But now that I have taken the GMAT, I can stress about the essays and recommendations, which will be crucial in convincing adcoms to take a chance on an applicant with so little relevant industry experience.

I believe anyone can prepare for the GMAT, and it doesn't cost a fortune. I found the official guide exceptional. Also, Bunuel shared some formulas that weren't in the guide and I had forgotten (such as the sum of the first n consecutive positive odd integers equals n squared). Practice is key! I wish you all luck in your business school endeavors and beyond!

Joined:<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Matrix` class that supports basic matrix operations: addition, subtraction, and multiplication. The class should handle matrices of different dimensions appropriately and include error handling for invalid operations. Your implementation should include the following methods:

- `__init__(self, data)`: Initializes the matrix with the given 2D list `data`. Raises a `ValueError` if `data` is not a valid 2D list with consistent row lengths.
- `__add__(self, other)`: Adds two matrices. Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
- `__sub__(self, other)`: Subtracts one matrix from another. Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
- `__mul__(self, other)`: Multiplies two matrices or a matrix by a scalar. Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices cannot be multiplied or if `other` is not a matrix or a scalar.
- `__repr__(self)`: Returns a string representation of the matrix.

**Sample Input:**
```python
matrix1 = Matrix([[1, 2], [3, 4]])
matrix2 = Matrix([[2, 0], [1, 2]])
print("Matrix 1:\n", matrix1)
print("Matrix 2:\n", matrix2)
print("Addition:\n", matrix1 + matrix2)
print("Subtraction:\n", matrix1 - matrix2)
print("Multiplication:\n", matrix1 * matrix2)
print("Scalar Multiplication with 3:\n", matrix1 * 3)
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Matrix 1:
 1 2
 3 4
Matrix 2:
 2 0
 1 2
Addition:
 3 2
 4 6
Subtraction:
 -1 2
 2 2
Multiplication:
 4 4
 10 8
Scalar Multiplication with 3:
 3 6
 9 12
```

```python
class Matrix:
    def __init__(self, data):
        """
        Initializes the matrix with the given 2D list `data`.
        Raises a `ValueError` if `data` is not a valid 2D list with consistent row lengths.
        """
        if not data or not all(len(row) == len(data[0]) for row in data):
            raise ValueError("Data must be a 2D list with consistent row lengths.")
        self.data = data
        self.rows = len(data)
        self.cols = len(data[0])

    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Adds two matrices.
        Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
        """
        if self.rows != other.rows or self.cols != other.cols:
            raise ValueError("Matrices must have the same dimensions for addition.")
        return Matrix([[self.data[i][j] + other.data[i][j] for j in range(self.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)])

    def __sub__(self, other):
        """
        Subtracts one matrix from another.
        Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
        """
        if self.rows != other.rows or self.cols != other.cols:
            raise ValueError("Matrices must have the same dimensions for subtraction.")
        return Matrix([[self.data[i][j] - other.data[i][j] for j in range(self.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)])

    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Multiplies two matrices or a matrix by a scalar.
        Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices cannot be multiplied or if `other` is not a matrix or a scalar.
        """
        if isinstance(other, Matrix):
            if self.cols != other.rows:
                raise ValueError("Number of columns in first matrix must equal number of rows in second matrix for multiplication.")
            result_matrix = [
                [sum(self.data[i][k] * other.data[k][j] for k in range(self.cols)) for j in range(other.cols)]
                for i in range(self.rows)
            ]
            return Matrix(result_matrix)
        elif isinstance(other, (int, float)):
            return Matrix([[self.data[i][j] * other for j in range(self.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)])
        else:
            raise ValueError("Multiplication is only defined between two matrices or a matrix and a scalar.")

    def __repr__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the matrix.
        """
        return '\n'.join([' '.join(map(str, row)) for row in self.data])

Display posts from previous: Sort by<#sep#>You are given a dataset representing customer purchase history. The dataset is structured as a list of lists, where each sublist contains three elements: `[customer_id, product_id, purchase_date]`. Your task is to generate a report for each customer. The report should include the total number of unique products purchased by the customer and the earliest and latest purchase dates. 

**Input:**
- A list of lists `dataset` where each sublist is of the form `[customer_id, product_id, purchase_date]`. 
- `purchase_date` is a string in the format `YYYY-MM-DD`.

**Output:**
- A dictionary where the keys are `customer_id` and the values are dictionaries containing:
  - `total_unique_products`: An integer representing the number of unique products purchased by the customer.
  - `purchase_dates`: A tuple of two strings representing the earliest and latest purchase dates in the format `YYYY-MM-DD`.

**Sample Input:**
```python
dataset = [
    [1, 101, '2021-01-01'],
    [1, 102, '2021-01-05'],
    [2, 101, '2021-01-02'],
    [1, 101, '2021-01-03'],
    [2, 103, '2021-01-01'],
    [1, 104, '2021-01-07'],
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    1: {'total_unique_products': 3, 'purchase_dates': ('2021-01-01', '2021-01-07')},
    2: {'total_unique_products': 2, 'purchase_dates': ('2021-01-01', '2021-01-02')}
}
```

```python
from datetime import datetime

def generate_customer_purchase_report(dataset):
    """
    Generates a purchase report for each customer based on the provided dataset.
    
    Parameters:
    dataset (list of lists): Each sublist contains [customer_id, product_id, purchase_date].
    
    Returns:
    dict: A dictionary with customer_id as keys and a dictionary of report details as values.
    """
    report = {}

    for customer_id, product_id, purchase_date in dataset:
        purchase_date = datetime.strptime(purchase_date, '%Y-%m-%d')

        if customer_id not in report:
            report[customer_id] = {
                'total_unique_products': set(),
                'purchase_dates': (purchase_date, purchase_date)
            }

        report[customer_id]['total_unique_products'].add(product_id)

        current_earliest, current_latest = report[customer_id]['purchase_dates']
        report[customer_id]['purchase_dates'] = (
            min(current_earliest, purchase_date),
            max(current_latest, purchase_date)
        )

    for customer_id, data in report.items():
        data['total_unique_products'] = len(data['total_unique_products'])
        data['purchase_dates'] = tuple(date.strftime('%Y-%m-%d') for date in data['purchase_dates'])

    return report
```<#sep#>*(9 -4)=6*5=30

273,6<#sep#>human: Let \$Z\$ be either the integers, the positive integers, or the non-zero integers; pick whatever's convenient. Give two functions \$f\$ and \$g\$, each \$Z \to Z\$, such that:

\$f(g(a)) = g(f(a))\$, for infinitely many integers \$a\$, and
\$f(g(b)) \ne g(f(b))\$, for infinitely many integers \$b\$.

Rules
Many of the rules from f(g(x)) decreases while g(f(x)) increases are stolen here:

Please provide an argument for why your functions fulfill the requirements.
Interpret "function" in the mathematical sense. That is, they must be total (defined over all of \$Z\$) and pure (given the same input, it must produce the same output).
You may submit the functions as a single program or two separate ones.

A single program must either have two named functions \$f\$ and \$g\$, or, when run, have output that can be interpreted as a pair of two functions \$f\$ and \$g\$.
Two separate programs must have one give \$f\$, and the other give \$g\$. The two programs may not refer to each other. In this case, functions may be unnamed.


Standard submission rules and input/output methods apply, but we require that \$f\$ and \$g\$ must have identical input and output representations; that is, they can be directly composed without manually converting in between. Conceptually, they must be functions over integers, so you can't cheat by using two different string representations of the same number or anything like that.
The usual integer overflow rules apply: your solution must be able to work for arbitrarily large integers in a hypothetical (or perhaps real) version of your language in which all integers are unbounded by default, but if your program fails in practice due to the implementation not supporting integers that large, that doesn't invalidate the solution.
This is code-golf. If you submit a single program, your score is the number of bytes of that program. If you submit two programs, your score is the sum of the number of bytes of both programs.

4. Calculus

Spivak, Michael

2006-01-01

Spivak's celebrated textbook is widely held as one of the finest introductions to mathematical analysis. His aim is to present calculus as the first real encounter with mathematics: it is the place to learn how logical reasoning combined with fundamental concepts can be developed into a rigorous mathematical theory rather than a bunch of tools and techniques learned by rote. Since analysis is a subject students traditionally find difficult to grasp, Spivak provides leisurely explanations, a profusion of examples, a wide range of exercises and plenty of illustrations in an easy-going approach that enlightens difficult concepts and rewards effort. Calculus will continue to be regarded as a modern classic, ideal for honours students and mathematics majors, who seek an alternative to doorstop textbooks on calculus, and the more formidable introductions to real analysis.

12. Continuation calculus

Bram Geron

2013-09-01

Full Text Available Programs with control are usually modeled using lambda calculus extended with control operators. Instead of modifying lambda calculus, we consider a different model of computation. We introduce continuation calculus, or CC, a deterministic model of computation that is evaluated using only head reduction, and argue that it is suitable for modeling programs with control. It is demonstrated how to define programs, specify them, and prove them correct. This is shown in detail by presenting in CC a list multiplication program that prematurely returns when it encounters a zero. The correctness proof includes termination of the program. In continuation calculus we can model both call-by-name and call-by-value. In addition, call-by-name functions can be applied to call-by-value results, and conversely.

15. CLEP calculus

Hill, Gregory

2013-01-01

Earn College Credit with REA's Test Prep for CLEP* Calculus Everything you need to pass the exam and get the college credit you deserve.Our test prep for CLEP* Calculus and the free online tools that come with it, will allow you to create a personalized CLEP* study plan that can be customized to fit you: your schedule, your learning style, and your current level of knowledge.Here's how it works:Diagnostic exam<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Bookshelf` class that manages a collection of books. Each book is represented by a `Book` object with attributes `title`, `author`, and `isbn`. Your `Bookshelf` class should support the following operations:

- **add_book(book)**: Adds a `Book` object to the collection.
- **remove_book_by_isbn(isbn)**: Removes the `Book` object with the specified `ISBN` from the collection.
- **get_books_by_author(author)**: Returns a list of `Book` objects written by the specified author.

**Constraints:**
- The `Book` class should have a constructor that initializes the `title`, `author`, and `isbn` attributes.
- The `Bookshelf` class should have a constructor that initializes an empty list of books.
- The `add_book` method should only add objects of type `Book`.
- The `remove_book_by_isbn` method should remove books based on their `isbn`.
- The `get_books_by_author` method should return a list of books by the specified author.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book1 = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935")
book2 = Book("Animal Farm", "George Orwell", "9780451526342")
book3 = Book("To Kill a Mockingbird", "Harper Lee", "9780060935467")

shelf = Bookshelf()
shelf.add_book(book1)
shelf.add_book(book2)
shelf.add_book(book3)

print(shelf.get_books_by_author("George Orwell"))  # Output: [<__main__.Book object at ...>, <__main__.Book object at ...>]
shelf.remove_book_by_isbn("9780451524935")
print(shelf.get_books_by_author("George Orwell"))  # Output: [<__main__.Book object at ...>]
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn):
        """
        Initializes a new Book object with the given title, author, and ISBN.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn

class Bookshelf:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes a new Bookshelf object with an empty list of books.
        """
        self.books = []

    def add_book(self, book):
        """
        Adds a Book object to the bookshelf if the input is a valid Book object.
        
        :param book: The Book object to be added.
        """
        if isinstance(book, Book):
            self.books.append(book)

    def remove_book_by_isbn(self, isbn):
        """
        Removes the Book object with the specified ISBN from the bookshelf.
        
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book to be removed.
        """
        self.books = [book for book in self.books if book.isbn != isbn]

    def get_books_by_author(self, author):
        """
        Returns a list of Book objects written by the specified author.
        
        :param author: The name of the author.
        :return: A list of Book objects by the specified author.
        """
        return [book for book in self.books if book.author == author]
```<#sep#>. Yes, that's correct.

In contrast to the original definition of safety, our<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script that processes a CSV file containing employee data and generates a Markdown report. The CSV file includes the following columns: `employee_id`, `name`, `position`, `department`, and `salary`. Your script must produce a Markdown file with a table listing all employees sorted first by department and then by position. Additionally, the report should include a summary section that specifies the total number of employees and the average salary.

**Input:**
- A CSV file named `employees.csv` with the following structure:
  ```
  employee_id,name,position,department,salary
  1,John Doe,Manager,HR,75000
  2,Jane Smith,Analyst,Finance,60000
  3,Bob Johnson,Manager,Finance,80000
  4,Alice Brown,Analyst,HR,55000
  ```

**Output:**
- A Markdown file named `report.md` with the following content:
  ```
  | Employee ID | Name        | Position | Department | Salary |
  |-------------|-------------|----------|------------|--------|
  | 4           | Alice Brown | Analyst  | HR         | 55000  |
  | 1           | John Doe    | Manager  | HR         | 75000  |
  | 2           | Jane Smith  | Analyst  | Finance    | 60000  |
  | 3           | Bob Johnson | Manager  | Finance    | 80000  |

  ## Summary
  - Total Employees: 4
  - Average Salary: 67500.00
  ```

```python
import csv
from collections import defaultdict

def generate_markdown_report(csv_file_path, markdown_report_path):
    """
    Generates a Markdown report from a CSV file containing employee data.
    
    Parameters:
    csv_file_path (str): The path to the input CSV file.
    markdown_report_path (str): The path to the output Markdown report file.
    """
    # Read CSV data
    employees = []
    with open(csv_file_path, mode='r') as file:
        reader = csv.DictReader(file)
        for row in reader:
            employees.append(row)

    # Sort employees by department and then by position
    employees.sort(key=lambda x: (x['department'], x['position']))

    # Create departments grouped data
    departments = defaultdict(list)
    total_salary = 0
    for employee in employees:
        departments[employee['department']].append(employee)
        total_salary += float(employee['salary'])

    # Write Markdown report
    with open(markdown_report_path, mode='w') as file:
        # Write table header
        file.write(f"| Employee ID | Name | Position | Department | Salary |\n")
        file.write(f"|-------------|------|----------|------------|--------|\n")

        # Write sorted employees
        for department in departments:
            for employee in departments[department]:
                file.write(f"| {employee['employee_id']} | {employee['name']} | {employee['position']} | {employee['department']} | {employee['salary']} |\n")
        
        # Write summary section
        total_employees = len(employees)
        average_salary = total_salary / total_employees if total_employees > 0 else 0
        file.write(f"\n## Summary\n")
        file.write(f"- Total Employees: {total_employees}\n")
        file.write(f"- Average Salary: {average_salary:.2f}\n")

# Example usage
generate_markdown_report('employees.csv', 'report.md')

9. The Safe Lambda Calculus

Blum, William

2009-01-01

Safety is a syntactic condition of higher-order grammars that constrains occurrences of variables in the production rules according to their type-theoretic order. In this paper, we introduce the safe lambda calculus, which is obtained by transposing (and generalizing) the safety condition to the setting of the simply-typed lambda calculus. In contrast to the original definition of safety, our calculus does not constrain types (to be homogeneous). We show that in the safe lambda calculus, there is no need to rename bound variables when performing substitution, as variable capture is guaranteed not to happen. We also propose an adequate notion of beta-reduction that preserves safety. In the same vein as Schwichtenberg's 1976 characterization of the simply-typed lambda calculus, we show that the numeric functions representable in the safe lambda calculus are exactly the multivariate polynomials; thus conditional is not definable. We also give a characterization of representable word functions. We then study the ...

4. Calculus of variations

Elsgolc, L E; Stark, M

1961-01-01

Calculus of Variations aims to provide an understanding of the basic notions and standard methods of the calculus of variations, including the direct methods of solution of the variational problems. The wide variety of applications of variational methods to different fields of mechanics and technology has made it essential for engineers to learn the fundamentals of the calculus of<#sep#>Implement a `CircularQueue` class in Python that supports the following operations:
- `enqueue(data)`: Adds an element to the end of the queue. If the queue is full, print "Queue is full." and do nothing.
- `dequeue()`: Removes and returns the element from the front of the queue. If the queue is empty, print "Queue is empty." and return `None`.
- `is_full()`: Returns `True` if the queue is full, otherwise `False`.
- `is_empty()`: Returns `True` if the queue is empty, otherwise `False`.

The queue should be circular, meaning that once the end of the underlying array is reached, the next element is added at the beginning of the array.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
cq = CircularQueue(3)
cq.enqueue(1)  # Queue: [1, None, None]
cq.enqueue(2)  # Queue: [1, 2, None]
cq.enqueue(3)  # Queue: [1, 2, 3]
cq.enqueue(4)  # Output: Queue is full.
cq.dequeue()   # Output: 1, Queue: [None, 2, 3]
cq.enqueue(4)  # Queue: [4, 2, 3]
cq.dequeue()   # Output: 2, Queue: [4, None, 3]
cq.dequeue()   # Output: 3, Queue: [4, None, None]
cq.dequeue()   # Output: Queue is empty.
cq.is_empty()  # Output: True
cq.is_full()   # Output: False
```

```python
class CircularQueue:
    def __init__(self, size):
        """
        Initialize the CircularQueue with a fixed size.
        :param size: The maximum number of elements the queue can hold.
        """
        self.size = size
        self.queue = [None] * size
        self.head = self.tail = -1

    def is_full(self):
        """
        Check if the queue is full.
        :return: True if the queue is full, False otherwise.
        """
        return (self.tail + 1) % self.size == self.head

    def is_empty(self):
        """
        Check if the queue is empty.
        :return: True if the queue is empty, False otherwise.
        """
        return self.head == -1

    def enqueue(self, data):
        """
        Add an element to the end of the queue.
        :param data: The element to be added to the queue.
        """
        if self.is_full():
            print("Queue is full.")
            return
        elif self.head == -1:  # First element in the queue
            self.head = 0
            self.tail = 0
        else:
            self.tail = (self.tail + 1) % self.size
        self.queue[self.tail] = data

    def dequeue(self):
        """
        Remove and return the element from the front of the queue.
        :return: The element at the front of the queue, or None if the queue is empty.
        """
        if self.is_empty():
            print("Queue is empty.")
            return None
        data = self.queue[self.head]
        if self.head == self.tail:  # Only one element in the queue
            self.head = -1
            self.tail = -1
        else:
            self.head = (self.head + 1) % self.size
        return data
```<#sep#>Given two square trinomials \( f(x) \) and \( g(x) \), and real numbers \( a, b, c, d \) such that \( f(a) = 2 \), \( f(b) = 3 \), \( f(c) = 7 \), \( f(d) = 10 \), and \( g(a) = 16 \), \( g(b) = 15 \), \( g(c) = 11 \), find \( g(d) \).

4. Calculus of variations

Elsgolc, L E; Stark, M

1961-01-01

Calculus of Variations aims to provide an understanding of the basic notions and standard methods of the calculus of variations, including the direct methods of solution of the variational problems. The wide variety of applications of variational methods to different fields of mechanics and technology has made it essential for engineers to learn the fundamentals of the calculus of variations. The book begins with a discussion of the method of variation in problems with fixed boundaries. Subsequent chapters cover variational problems with movable boundaries and some other problems; sufficiency

5. A Logical Process Calculus

Cleaveland, Rance; Luettgen, Gerald; Bushnell, Dennis M. (Technical Monitor)

2002-01-01

This paper presents the Logical Process Calculus (LPC), a formalism that supports heterogeneous system specifications containing both operational and declarative subspecifications. Syntactically, LPC extends Milner's Calculus of Communicating Systems with operators from the alternation-free linear-time mu-calculus (LT(mu)). Semantically, LPC is equipped with a behavioral preorder that generalizes Hennessy's and DeNicola's must-testing preorder as well as LT(mu's) satisfaction relation, while being compositional for all LPC operators. From a technical point of view, the new calculus is distinguished by the inclusion of: (1) both minimal and maximal fixed-point operators and (2) an unimple-mentability predicate on process terms, which tags inconsistent specifications. The utility of LPC is demonstrated by means of an example highlighting the benefits of heterogeneous system specification.

9. Disappearing renal calculus

Cui, Helen; Thomas, Johanna; Kumar, Sunil

2013-01-01

We present a case of a renal calculus treated solely with antibiotics which has not been previously reported in the literature. A man with a 17 mm lower pole renal calculus and concurrent Escherichia coli urine infection was being worked up to undergo percutaneous nephrolithotomy. However, after a course of preoperative antibiotics the stone was no longer seen on retrograde pyelography or CT imaging.

11. Symmetric π—Calculus

傅育熙

1998-01-01

An alternative presentation of the π－calculus is given.This version of the π-calculus is symmetric in the sense that communications are symmetric and there is no difference between input and output prefixes.The point of the symmetric π-calculus is that it has no abstract names.The set of closed names is therefore homogeneous.The π－calculus can be fully embedded into the symmetric π-calculus.The symmetry changes the emphasis of the communication mechanism of the π-calculus and opens up possibility for further variations.

12. Proof nets for the Displacement calculus

Moot, Richard

2016-01-01

We present a proof net calculus for the Displacement calculus and show its correctness. This is the first proof net calculus which models the Displacement calculus directly and not by some sort of translation into another formalism. The proof net calculus opens up new possibilities for parsing and proof search with the Displacement calculus.

16. Cirquent calculus deepened

Japaridze, Giorgi

2007-01-01

Cirquent calculus is a new proof-theoretic framework, originally motivited by the needs of computability logic (see http://www.cis.upenn.edu/~giorgi/cl.html ). Its main distinguishing feature is sharing: unlike the more traditional frameworks that manipulate tree- or forest-like objects such as formulas, sequents or hypersequents, cirquent calculus deals with circuit-style structures called cirquents. The present article elaborates a deep-inference cirquent calculus system CL8 for classical propositional logic and the corresponding fragment of the resource-conscious computability logic. It also shows the existence of polynomial-size analytic CL8-proofs of the pigeonhole principle -- the family of tautologies known to have no such proofs in traditional systems.

Although individual economic decisions are generally made at discrete time intervals, they may well be less than perfectly synchronized in ways discrete models postulate. On the other hand, the usual assumption that economic activity takes place continuously, is nothing else than a convenient abstraction that in many applications is far from reality. The Hahn quantum calculus helps to bridge the gap between the two families of models: continuous and discrete. Quantum Variational Calculus is self-contained and unified in presentation. 18. Calculus of variations

Gelfand, I<#sep#>You are given a list of sentences. Your task is to group these sentences into clusters based on their semantic similarity using the cosine similarity metric. Implement a Python script that performs this task. Use pre-trained embeddings from `nltk` or any other library. The script should output the indices of the sentences in each cluster. Assume that the number of clusters is 3.

**Input:**
- A list of sentences.

**Output:**
- A list of clusters, where each cluster is a list of indices of sentences in the original list.

**Sample Input:**
```python
sentences = [
    "The cat sat on the mat.",
    "A cat is sitting on a mat.",
    "The dog is running in the park.",
    "A dog runs in a park.",
    "The bird flew high in the sky."
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Cluster 0: [0, 1]
Cluster 1: [2, 3]
Cluster 2: [4]
```

```python
import nltk
import numpy as np
from nltk.corpus import wordnet as wn
from sklearn.cluster import AgglomerativeClustering
from sklearn.metrics.pairwise import cosine_similarity
import warnings
warnings.filterwarnings('ignore', category=DeprecationWarning)

nltk.download('wordnet')
nltk.download('omw-1.4')

def get_word_vector(word):
    """
    Returns a vector representing a word based on WordNet embeddings.
    
    Args:
        word (str): The word to convert to a vector.
        
    Returns:
        np.ndarray: The vector representation of the word.
    """
    synsets = wn.synsets(word)
    if synsets:
        synset = synsets[0]
        return np.array(synset.vector())
    else:
        return np.zeros(300)

def sentence_to_vector(sentence):
    """
    Represents a sentence as a mean vector sum of its word embeddings.
    
    Args:
        sentence (str): The input sentence to convert.
        
    Returns:
        np.ndarray: The mean vector representation of the sentence.
    """
    words = nltk.word_tokenize(sentence.lower())
    words_vector = [get_word_vector(word) for word in words]
    sentence_vector = np.mean(words_vector, axis=0)
    return sentence_vector

def group_sentences(sentences):
    """
    Groups sentences into clusters based on their semantic similarity.
    
    Args:
        sentences (list): A list of sentences to cluster.
        
    Returns:
        list: A list of clusters; each cluster is a list of indices of sentences in the original list.
    """
    sentence_vectors = np.array([sentence_to_vector(sent) for sent in sentences])
    sim_matrix = cosine_similarity(sentence_vectors)
    num_clusters = 3
    clustering_model = AgglomerativeClustering(n_clusters=num_clusters, affinity='precomputed', linkage='complete')
    cluster_labels = clustering_model.fit_predict(1 - sim_matrix)
    clusters = [[] for _ in range(num_clusters)]
    for i, label in enumerate(cluster_labels):
        clusters[label].append(i)
    return clusters

# Example usage
sentences = [
    "The cat sat on the mat.",
    "A cat is sitting on a mat.",
    "The dog is running in the park.",
    "A dog runs in a park.",
    "The bird flew high in the sky."
]

clusters = group_sentences(sentences)

for i, cluster in enumerate(clusters):
    print(f"Cluster {i}: {cluster}")

5. The simply typed rewriting calculus

Cirstea, Horatiu; Kirchner, Claude

2000-01-01

The rewriting calculus is a rule construction and application framework. As such it embeds in a uniform way term rewriting and lambda-calculus. Since rule application is an explicit object of the calculus, it allows us also to handle the set of results explicitly. We present a simply typed version of the rewriting calculus. With a good choice of the type system, we show that the calculus is type preserving and terminating, i.e. verifies the subject reduction and strong normalization properties.

Several<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python. The class should support the following operations:
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. The amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance.
- **Check Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.
- **Transfer**: Transfer a specified amount from the current account to another `BankAccount` instance. The amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance.

The class should have the following attributes:
- `account_holder_name`: A string representing the name of the account holder.
- `balance`: A float representing the current balance of the account, initialized to 0.0 if not provided.

For each operation, if the input is invalid (e.g., negative amount, insufficient funds), the method should raise a `ValueError` with an appropriate message.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account1 = BankAccount("Alice", 1000)
account2 = BankAccount("Bob", 500)
account1.deposit(500)  # Balance of account1 should be 1500
account1.withdraw(200)  # Balance of account1 should be 1300
account1.check_balance()  # Should return 1300
account1.transfer(account2, 300)  # Balance of account1 should be 1000, balance of account2 should be 800
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_holder_name, initial_balance=0.0):
        """
        Initialize a new BankAccount instance.

        :param account_holder_name: The name of the account holder.
        :param initial_balance: The initial balance of the account, default is 0.0.
        """
        self.account_holder_name = account_holder_name
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: The amount to deposit, must be positive.
        :return: The updated balance of the account.
        :raises ValueError: If the amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive")
        self.balance += amount
        return self.balance

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.

        :param amount: The amount to withdraw, must be positive and not exceed the current balance.
        :return: The updated balance of the account.
        :raises ValueError: If the amount is not positive or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for this withdrawal")
        self.balance -= amount
        return self.balance

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check the current balance of the account.

        :return: The current balance of the account.
        """
        return self.balance

    def transfer(self, target_account, amount):
        """
        Transfer a specified amount from the current account to another BankAccount instance.

        :param target_account: The target BankAccount instance to transfer funds to.
        :param amount: The amount to transfer, must be positive and not exceed the current balance.
        :return: A tuple containing the updated balances of the current and target accounts.
        :raises ValueError: If the target account is not a BankAccount instance, the amount is not positive, or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if not isinstance(target_account, BankAccount):
            raise ValueError("Target account must be an instance of BankAccount")
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Transfer amount must be positive")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for this transfer")
        
        self.withdraw(amount)
        target_account.deposit(amount)
        return self.balance, target_account.balance
```<#sep#> = p c² + q c + r = 7,

f(d) = p d² + q d + r =10.

Similarly, for g(x) = s x² + t x + u,

g(a) = s a² + t a + u = 16,

g(b) = s b² + t b + u =15,

g(c) = s c² + t c + u =11,

and we need to find g(d).

Since both f and g are quadratic, their coefficients can be determined by three points each, but here, the four points (a,b,c,d) are shared between f and g, except that g is missing the value at d. The problem is to find g(d). Since the problem gives f(d)=10, maybe there is a way to set up a system that relates f and g through these equations?

Alternatively, let's think of the problem as overdetermined for f but perhaps the fourth equation (for d) gives a condition that can be used.

Alternatively, since both quadratics pass through four points with some conditions, maybe we can set up equations such that the difference between g and some multiple of f gives a linear function?

Alternatively, construct the system of equations for f and see if we can relate it to g.

Alternatively, since the problem gives four values for f and three for g, perhaps forming equations for g in terms of f's equations?

Alternatively, maybe subtract the equations for f and g in some way. Let me think. Suppose I form equations for g minus k times f:

g(x) - k f(x) = (s - kp) x² + (t - kq)x + (u - kr).

If we can choose k such that this is a linear function (i.e., eliminate the x² term), then that might allow us to solve for the remaining terms. Since we know some values, this could work.

Suppose that s = k p, so the x² term cancels out. Then the resulting function would be linear. Let's try this idea.

Set k = s/p (assuming p ≠ 0, which it is since it's a quadratic). Then the coefficient of x² becomes zero. Then the function would be linear, and so can be determined by two points. Let's see if with the given data, this can be done.

But to find such a k, maybe with the given data points?

Alternatively, pick two equations for f and g and see if there's a linear combination.

Alternatively, since we know three points for both f and g, let's set up the equations for f and g and see if we can find a relationship.

Alternatively, since the previous approach worked so neatly with the linear combination, perhaps that is the intended path. The fact that the three points for g lie on a straight line when plotted against f(x) suggests that g is a linear function of f. Since that worked for the given points, then the value at d can be extrapolated.

g(a) = 16, and f(a)=2,

g(b)=15, f(b)=3,

g(c)=11, f(c)=7.

Assuming g = m*f + n,

Equation 1: 2m + n =16,

Equation 2: 3m + n =15,

Subtract 1-2: -m =1 ⇒ m = -1,

Then, n = 16 -2*(-1)=16+2=18.

Check third equation: 7*(-1)+18 = -7+18=11, which matches.

Thus, for f(d)=10,

g(d)= -1*(10) +18=8.

So unless there's some hidden constraint I missed, like the functions being square trinomials imposes some condition, this should be correct.

Wait, but what's the deal with "square trinomials"? Did I miss that? The problem says "Given two square trinomials f(x) and g(x)".

If "square trinomial" means that they are perfect squares, then f(x) = (mx + n)^2 and g(x)=(px + q)^2, so they are quadratics with discriminant zero (since they are squares). Let me see if this holds with the previous assumption.

Suppose that f(x) is a perfect square. Then f(x) = (mx + n)^2. Then, since f(a)=2, (ma +n)^2=2; similarly f(b)=3, so (mb +n)^2=3, etc. Similarly, g(x)= (px + q)^2, so (pa + q)^2=16, (pb + q)^2=15, etc.

But in that case, the relationship between g and f might not be linear, but in this case, the previous approach gave us g(x)= -f(x)+18, but if f(x) is a perfect square, then g(x)= - (mx +n)^2 +18. But g(x) is also supposed to be a perfect square. Therefore, we have:

- (mx +n)^2 +18 = (px + q)^2.

Let me see if that can be possible. Let me rearrange:

(px + q)^2 + (mx +n)^2 = 18.

Hmm, that would require that the sum of two squares is a constant 18. That's possible only if both squares are constants themselves, but unless their variables cancel out. Wait, but unless mx +n and px + q are linear functions whose squares add to 18. So, for all x, (mx +n)^2 + (px + q)^2 =18. But this is only possible if the coefficients of x in both terms are zero, so that they are constants. Which would require m=0 and p=0, but then they wouldn't be quadratic functions. Contradiction.

Therefore, this suggests that my initial assumption that g is a linear function of f might not hold if f and g are perfect squares. Therefore, perhaps "square trinomials" don't mean perfect squares but just quadratic trinomials? Because otherwise, there is a contradiction.

Alternatively, maybe "square trinomial" refers to trinomials that can be arranged into squares, but they are still quadratics in general form. But in standard terminology, a square trinomial is a quadratic that is a perfect square, so the problem might have meant that. Hmm, this is a critical point. Alternatively, perhaps the problem uses "square trinomial" to mean quadratic trinomials, not necessarily perfect squares, and the word "square" is redundant or a mistranslation. Since the problem seems solvable under the linear relationship, maybe that is the case.

Alternatively, let me see if there is a different approach assuming that f and g are perfect squares.

But then, these equations would have to hold for some constants m and n. But with four different points, this might be impossible.

Alternatively, since f has four different values at four different points, unless each pair (x, f(x)) corresponds to different branches of the square root, this would require different combinations. But the equations would need to be consistent, which seems complicated.

Alternatively, perhaps f(x) is not a perfect square over all real numbers, but just a trinomial that is a square for specific x values? That doesn't quite make sense.

Alternatively, the problem might just mean "quadratic trinomials", since quadratics are degree two trinomials (with three terms). Maybe "square" is redundant, but in any case, the key is that they are quadratics.

Assuming that, then my first approach holds, giving g(d)=8.

bst.insert(30)
bst.insert(20)
bst.insert(40)
bst.insert(70)
bst.insert(60)
bst.insert(80)
print(bst.inorder_traversal())  # Output: [20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80]
print(bst.search(40))          # Output: <TreeNode object with val=40>
print(bst.search(90))          # Output: None
bst.delete(20)
print(bst.inorder_traversal())  # Output: [30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80]
bst.delete(30)
print(bst.inorder_traversal())  # Output: [40, 50, 60, 70, 80]
bst.delete(50)
print(bst.inorder_traversal())  # Output: [40, 60, 70, 80]
```

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.val = key

class BinarySearchTree:
    def __init__(self):
        self.root = None

    def insert(self, key):
        """Insert a new node with the given key into the BST."""
        if self.root is None:
            self.root = TreeNode(key)
        else:
            self._insert_recursively(self.root, key)

    def _insert_recursively(self, node, key):
        if key < node.val:
            if node.left is None:
                node.left = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self._insert_recursively(node.left, key)
        elif key > node.val:
            if node.right is None:
                node.right = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self._insert_recursively(node.right, key)

    def search(self, key):
        """Search for a node with the given key in the BST."""
        return self._search_recursively(self.root, key)

    def _search_recursively(self, node, key):
        if node is None or node.val == key:
            return node
        if key < node.val:
            return self._search_recursively(node.left, key)
        return self._search_recursively(node.right, key)

    def delete(self, key):
        """Delete the node with the given key from the BST."""
        self.root = self._delete_recursively(self.root, key)

    def _delete_recursively(self, node, key):
        if node is None:
            return node

        if key < node.val:
            node.left = self._delete_recursively(node.left, key)
        elif key > node.val:
            node.right = self._delete_recursively(node.right, key)
        else:
            if node.left is None:
                return node.right
            elif node.right is None:
                return node.left

            node.val = self._min_value_node(node.right).val
            node.right = self._delete_recursively(node.right, node.val)

        return node

    def _min_value_node(self, node):
        """Return the node with the minimum value in the subtree rooted at node."""
        current = node
        while current.left is not None:
            current = current.left
        return current

    def inorder_traversal(self):
        """Return the keys in the BST in sorted order using in-order traversal."""
        return self._inorder_traversal_recursively(self.root)

    def _inorder_traversal_recursively(self, node):
        res = []
        if node is not None:
            res = self._inorder_traversal_recursively(node.left)
            res.append(node.val)
            res = res + self._inorder_traversal_recursively(node.right)
        return res
```<#sep#>2

2) p b² + q b + r = 3

3) p c² + q c + r =7

4) p d² + q d + r =10

Similarly, for g(x):

5) s a² + t a + u =16

6) s b² + t b + u =15

7) s c² + t c + u =11

And we need to find 8) s d² + t d + u = ?

We can set up a system here. Let me subtract equations of f and g to eliminate variables.

But maybe another approach: Let’s consider the equations for g and f. Let’s suppose that there is a linear relation between g and f, so g = m f + n, as before. Let me write that:

Equation 5: s a² + t a + u = m*(p a² + q a + r) + n = m*f(a) + n = m*2 +n =16

Similarly, 6: m*3 +n =15

7: m*7 +n=11

Which leads us to m=-1, n=18, so then equation 8 would be g(d)= -10 +18=8, as before.

But if that relationship holds, then indeed, the coefficients of g must satisfy:

s x² + t x + u = m*(p x² + q x + r) +n

Thus, equating coefficients:

s = m*p,

t = m*q,

u = m*r +n.

Therefore, even though we don’t know the coefficients of f, this relationship holds. Since the problem doesn’t require us to find f or g explicitly, but just g(d), which can be found through this linear relation, this is sufficient.

Therefore, even if f and g are quadratics, this linear relation between them is valid, given that it works for three points, so must work for all x. Hence the answer is 8.

Therefore, I think that answer is correct. The key was recognizing that g is a linear function of f, which works with the given data. The mention of "square trinomials" might just be emphasizing that they are quadratics (since a trinomial is a three-term polynomial, which a quadratic can be), and not restricting them to perfect squares. If they were perfect squares, then the linear relationship would lead to a contradiction, as I saw earlier, so perhaps "square trinomial" here is just another way of saying quadratic trinomial.

Thus, I will go with \boxed{8} as the answer.
</think>Given two square trinomials \( f(x) \) and \( g(x) \), we know the following values:
\[ f(a) = 2, \quad f(b) = 3, \quad f(c) = 7, \quad f(d) = 10 \]
\[ g(a) = 16, \quad g(b) = 15, \quad g(c) = 11 \]

We need to find \( g(d) \).

Assume that \( g(x) \) is a linear function of \( f(x) \). Therefore, we can write:
\[ g(x) = m \cdot f(x) + n \]

Using the given values, we set up the following system of equations:
1. \( g(a) = m \cdot f(a) + n = 16 \)
2. \( g(b) = m \cdot f(b) + n = 15 \)
3. \( g(c) = m \cdot f(c) + n = 11 \)

Substitute the values of \( f(a) \), \( f(b) \), and \( f(c) \) into these equations:
1. \( 2m + n = 16 \)
2. \( 3m + n = 15 \)
3. \( 7m + n = 11 \)

To find \( m \) and \( n \), we subtract the first equation from the second:
\[ (3m + n) - (2m + n) = 15 - 16 \]
\[ m = -1 \]

Now substitute \( m = -1 \) into the first equation:
\[ 2(-1) + n = 16 \]
\[ -2 + n = 16 \]
\[ n = 18 \]

To verify, substitute \( m = -1 \) and \( n = 18 \) into the third equation:
\[ 7(-1) + 18 = 11 \]
\[ -7 + 18 = 11 \]
This is correct.

Thus, the relationship between \( g(x) \) and \( f(x) \) is:
\[ g(x) = -f(x) + 18 \]

Using \( f(d) = 10 \):
\[ g(d) = -10 + 18 = 8 \]

Therefore, the value of \( g(d) \) is:
\[ \boxed{8} \]<#sep#>15 characters.

15. Rational Orthogonal Calculus

We show that one can use model categories to construct rational orthogonal calculus. 3. Pre-Calculus For Dummies

Kuang, Yang

2012-01-01

The fun<#sep#>You are tasked with creating a function `generate_sudoku` that generates a valid 9x9 Sudoku puzzle with a unique solution. The function should return the puzzle as a 2D list, where `0` represents an empty cell. The generated puzzle should have a unique solution and should be solvable.

**Sample Input:**
None

**Sample Output:**
```
[
 [5, 3, 0, 0, 7, 0, 0, 0, 0],
 [6, 0, 0, 1, 9, 5, 0, 0, 0],
 [0, 9, 8, 0, 0, 0, 0, 6, 0],
 [8, 0, 0, 0, 6, 0, 0, 0, 3],
 [4, 0, 0, 8, 0, 3, 0, 0, 1],
 [7, 0, 0, 0, 2, 0, 0, 0, 6],
 [0, 6, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2, 8, 0],
 [0, 0, 0, 4, 1, 9, 0, 0, 5],
 [0, 0, 0, 0, 8, 0, 0, 7, 9]
]
```

```python
import random

def is_valid(board, row, col, num):
    """
    Check if placing `num` at position (row, col) in the board is valid.
    """
    for i in range(9):
        if board[row][i] == num or board[i][col] == num:
            return False

    start_row, start_col = 3 * (row // 3), 3 * (col // 3)
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            if board[i + start_row][j + start_col] == num:
                return False
    return True

def solve_sudoku(board):
    """
    Solve the Sudoku puzzle using backtracking.
    """
    empty = find_empty_location(board)
    if not empty:
        return True  # Puzzle solved
    row, col = empty

    for num in range(1, 10):
        if is_valid(board, row, col, num):
            board[row][col] = num
            if solve_sudoku(board):
                return True
            board[row][col] = 0  # Backtrack
    return False

def find_empty_location(board):
    """
    Find an empty location (represented by 0) in the board.
    """
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if board[i][j] == 0:
                return (i, j)
    return None

def count_solutions(board, row_col):
    """
    Count the number of solutions for the Sudoku puzzle starting from position (row, col).
    """
    row, col = row_col
    num_solutions = 0
    for num in range(1, 10):
        if is_valid(board, row, col, num):
            board[row][col] = num
            if not find_empty_location(board):
                num_solutions += 1
            else:
                num_solutions += count_solutions(board, find_empty_location(board))
            board[row][col] = 0  # Backtrack
    return num_solutions

def generate_sudoku():
    """
    Generate a valid 9x9 Sudoku puzzle with a unique solution.
    """
    while True:
        # Create a solved Sudoku
        board = [[0] * 9 for _ in range(9)]
        numbers = list(range(1, 10))
        
        for i in range(9):
            random.shuffle(numbers)
            for j in range(9):
                if is_valid(board, i, j, numbers[j]):
                    board[i][j] = numbers[j]
                    break
            else:
                break  # If no valid number is found, restart
        else:
            break  # If the loop completes, the board is fully filled
        
        # Create the puzzle by removing numbers
        attempts = 5
        while attempts > 0:
            row, col = random.randint(0, 8), random.randint(0, 8)
            while board[row][col] == 0:
                row, col = random.randint(0, 8), random.randint(0, 8)
            num = board[row][col]
            board[row][col] = 0

            # Make a copy of the board to count the number of solutions
            temp_board = [row[:] for row in board]

            if find_empty_location(temp_board):
                num_solutions = count_solutions(temp_board, (row, col))
                if num_solutions != 1:
                    board[row][col] = num
                    attempts -= 1
        return board
```<#sep#>How many squares with sides parallel to the axes can be formed from a total of 64 dots arranged in an $8 \times 8$ grid?

To satisfy the problem's requirements, we can define two functions \( f \) and \( g \) over the integers that commute for infinitely many values but also fail to commute for infinitely many values. The chosen functions use simple parity-based logic to achieve this.

### Explanation:
- **Functions**:
  - \( f(x) = x \, \text{if even} \), else \( x - 1 \).
  - \( g(x) = x + 1 \, \text{if odd} \), else \( x \).
  
  - **Commutation Analysis**:
    - For **even \( a \)**:
      - \( g(a) \) is even, so \( f(g(a)) = g(a) \).
      - \( f(a) \) is even, so \( g(f(a)) = f(a) \).
      - Thus, \( f(g(a)) = g(f(a)) \) for all even integers \( a \).
    - For **odd \( a \)**:
      - \( f(g(a)) = f(a + 1) = a + 1 \) (since \( a + 1 \) is even).
      - \( g(f(a)) = g(a - 1) = (a - 1) - 1 \) (since \( a - 1 \) is even).
      - This results in different values, so \( f(g(a)) \ne g(f(a)) \) for all odd integers \( a \).

### Code (JavaScript):
Two separate programs:

**First Program (function \( f \))**:
```javascript
x=>x&-2
```

**Second Program (function \( g \))**:
```javascript
x=>x+x%2
```

These functions leverage bitwise operations and modulo arithmetic to efficiently split integers into even and odd cases, ensuring the desired commutation properties. The first program uses a bitwise AND with \(-2\) to floor odd numbers to even, while the second adds \(1\) to odd numbers using modulo \(2\).

### Final Answer:
The two functions are defined as follows in JavaScript:

Function \( f \):
```javascript
x=>x&-2
```

Function \( g \):
```javascript
x=>x+x%2
```

These programs ensure that the composition \( f(g(a)) \) equals \( g(f(a)) \) for all even \( a \), and they differ for all odd \( a \), satisfying the problem's conditions. The total byte count is 15, which is the sum of the two programs' byte counts.

4. Stochastic Calculus of Wrapped Compartments

Coppo, Mario; Drocco, Maurizio; Grassi, Elena; Troina, Angelo; 10.4204/EPTCS.28.6

2010-01-01

The Calculus of Wrapped Compartments (CWC) is a variant of the Calculus of Looping Sequences (CLS). While keeping the same expressiveness, CWC strongly simplifies the development of automatic tools for the analysis of biological systems. The main simplification consists in the removal of the sequencing operator, thus lightening the formal treatment of the patterns to be matched in a term (whose complexity in CLS is strongly affected by the variables matching in the sequences). We define a stochastic semantics for this new calculus. As an application we model the interaction between macrophages and apoptotic neutrophils and a mechanism of gene regulation in E.Coli.

6. Stochastic calculus with infinitesimals

Herzberg, Frederik

2013-01-01

Stochastic analysis is not only a thriving area of pure mathematics with intriguing connections to partial differential equations and differential geometry. It also has numerous applications in the natural and social sciences (for instance in financial mathematics or theoretical quantum mechanics) and therefore appears in physics and economics curricula as well. However, existing approaches to stochastic analysis either presuppose various concepts from measure theory and functional analysis or lack full mathematical rigour. This short book proposes to solve the dilemma: By adopting E. Nelson's "radically elementary" theory of continuous-time stochastic processes, it is based on a demonstrably consistent use of infinitesimals and thus permits a radically simplified, yet perfectly rigorous approach to stochastic calculus and its fascinating applications, some of which (notably the Black-Scholes theory of option pricing and the Feynman path integral) are also discussed in the book.

# Function: calculate_mean
# Category: Mathematical Calculation
# Explanation: This function calculates and returns the mean of a list of numbers, which is a common mathematical operation.
def calculate_mean(numbers):
    """Calculates and returns the mean of a list of numbers. Category: Mathematical Calculation."""
    return sum(numbers) / len(numbers)

# Function: print_file_contents
# Category: Input/Output
# Explanation: This function reads the contents of a file and prints it, involving file I/O operations.
def print_file_contents(filename):
    """Reads the contents of a file and prints it. Category: Input/Output."""
    with open(filename, 'r') as file:
        print(file.read())

# Function: is_even
# Category: Conditional Logic
# Explanation: This function checks if a number is even by using a conditional operation (modulus), returning a boolean result.
def is_even(number):
    """Checks if a number is even. Category: Conditional Logic."""
    return number % 2 == 0

# Function: filter_positive_numbers
# Category: Data Manipulation
# Explanation: This function filters a list of numbers to include only positive values, modifying the dataset.
def filter_positive_numbers(numbers):
    """Filters a list of numbers to include only positive values. Category: Data Manipulation."""
    return [num for num in numbers if num > 0]

# Function: iterate_over_list
# Category: Looping
# Explanation: This function iterates over a list, performing an action (printing each element), which is a typical use of a loop.
def iterate_over_list(numbers):
    """Iterates over a list and prints each element. Category: Looping."""
    for num in numbers:
        print(num)

```python
def calculate_mean(numbers):
    """Calculates and returns the mean of a list of numbers. Category: Mathematical Calculation."""
    return sum(numbers) / len(numbers)

def print_file_contents(filename):
    """Reads the contents of a file and prints it. Category: Input/Output."""
    with open(filename, 'r') as file:
        print(file.read())

def is_even(number):
    """Checks if a number is even. Category: Conditional Logic."""
    return number % 2 == 0

def filter_positive_numbers(numbers):
    """Filters a list of numbers to include only positive values. Category: Data Manipulation."""
    return [num for num in numbers if num > 0]

def iterate_over_list(numbers):
    """Iterates over a list and prints each element. Category: Looping."""
    for num in numbers:
        print(num)
```<#sep#>7) = 1 \times 1 = 1\).

3. **Summing the Squares**:
   The total number of squares is the sum of the squares of the integers from 1 to 7:
   \[
   1^2 + 2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2 + 5^2 + 6^2 + 7^2
   \]
   This sum can be calculated using the formula for the sum of squares of the first \(n\) positive integers:
   \[
   \sum_{k=1}^n k^2 = \frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}{6}
   \]
   For \(n = 7\):
   \[
   \sum_{k=1}^7 k^2 = \frac{7 \times 8 \times 15}{6} = \frac{840}{6} = 140
   \]

Therefore, the total number of axis-aligned squares in an \(8 \times 8\) grid of dots is \(\boxed{140}\).<#sep#>human: We all know run-length decoding: A list of character-length pairs expands to a string of that many characters, so A1B4C2 expands to ABBBBCC. But what if we stretch this concept to 2 dimensions?
Instead of character-length pairs, in this challenge you will be given character-length-direction triples. For example, using N/S/E/W to indicate direction, the input A4E B5S C3W D3N E1W should yield the output
AAAAB   
    B
 E  B
 D  B
 D  B
 DCCC

You can take input in any reasonable format (such as a list of (char, length, N/E/S/W) like shown here, or perhaps (0/1/2/3, length, char)).
The input will not overlap itself, so A5E B3W or A5E B4S C4W D4N is invalid input.
The input will also not expand past the left or top edges of the string, only the bottom and right edges will be expanded.
The character will always be within the printable ASCII range.
Consistent trailing or leading whitespace that does not affect the presentation of the output is fine.
This is code-golf, so the shortest submission per language, measured in bytes, wins.
Here are a couple cases to test your entry against:
A4E B5S C3W D3N E1W
AAAAB   
    B
 E  B
 D  B
 D  B
 DCCC

A4E B5S C3W D3N -2E -1N  2W |1S
AAAAB   
 |  B
 ---B
 D  B
 D  B
 DCCC

.3S  2E ,3N  2E .5S
.   . 
. , .  
. , .  
  , .   
    .

>6E v3S <6W ^2N >5E v1S <5W
>>>>>>v 
>>>>>vv
^<<<<<v
^<<<<<<

1994-01-01

A theory of integration for anticom<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a currency conversion function. Given an amount of money and two currency codes, your function should convert the amount from the source currency to the target currency using predefined exchange rates. The function should handle the following exchange rates as of 2023:

- USD to EUR: 0.85
- EUR to USD: 1.18
- USD to GBP: 0.75
- GBP to USD: 1.33
- EUR to GBP: 0.88
- GBP to EUR: 1.14

Your function should return the converted amount rounded to two decimal places. If the conversion rate between the given currencies is not available, return the string "Exchange rate not available for the given currencies."

**Input:**
- The function will be called with three parameters:
  - `amount`: A float representing the amount of money to be converted.
  - `from_currency`: A string representing the currency code of the source currency.
  - `to_currency`: A string representing the currency code of the target currency.

**Output:**
- A float representing the converted amount, rounded to two decimal places, or a string indicating that the exchange rate is not available.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- `convert_currency(100, 'USD', 'EUR')` should return `85.0`
- `convert_currency(50, 'EUR', 'GBP')` should return `44.0`
- `convert_currency(75, 'GBP', 'USD')` should return `100.13`
- `convert_currency(100, 'USD', 'JPY')` should return `"Exchange rate not available for the given currencies."`

```python
def convert_currency(amount, from_currency, to_currency):
    """
    Convert a given amount of money from one currency to another using predefined exchange rates.
    
    Parameters:
    - amount (float): The amount of money to be converted.
    - from_currency (str): The currency code of the source currency.
    - to_currency (str): The currency code of the target currency.
    
    Returns:
    - float: The converted amount rounded to two decimal places, or a string indicating that the exchange rate is not available.
    """
    # Define the exchange rates
    exchange_rates = {
        'USD': {'EUR': 0.85, 'GBP': 0.75},
        'EUR': {'USD': 1.18, 'GBP': 0.88},
        'GBP': {'USD': 1.33, 'EUR': 1.14}
    }
    
    # Direct conversion
    if from_currency == to_currency:
        return round(amount, 2)
    
    # Convert the amount using the defined exchange rates
    try:
        converted_amount = amount * exchange_rates[from_currency][to_currency]
        return round(converted_amount, 2)
    except KeyError:
        return "Exchange rate not available for the given currencies."

14. Stochastic Pi-calculus Revisited

Cardelli, Luca; Mardare, Radu Iulian

2013-01-01

We develop a version of stochastic Pi-calculus with a semantics based on<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple banking system using a `BankAccount` class. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Create an account** with an initial balance.
2. **Deposit** money into an account.
3. **Withdraw** money from an account.
4. **Check the balance** of an account.

The `BankAccount` class should have the following attributes:
- `account_number`: a unique identifier for the account (use a simple integer).
- `account_holder`: a string representing the name of the account holder.
- `balance`: a float representing the current balance of the account.

Your implementation should include methods for each of the operations listed above. The methods should handle invalid inputs gracefully.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `n` representing the number of operations.
- The next `n` lines describe the operations. Each operation is described by a command followed by parameters:
  - `CREATE account_number account_holder initial_balance`: Create a new account.
  - `DEPOSIT account_number amount`: Deposit money into the specified account.
  - `WITHDRAW account_number amount`: Withdraw money from the specified account.
  - `CHECK account_number`: Check the balance of the specified account.

**Output:**
- For each `DEPOSIT`, `WITHDRAW`, and `CHECK` operation, print the appropriate message as shown in the sample output.

**Sample Input:**
```
5
CREATE 12345 JohnDoe 1000.0
DEPOSIT 12345 500.0
WITHDRAW 12345 200.0
CHECK 12345
WITHDRAW 12345 1500.0
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Deposited $500.00. New balance: $1500.00
Withdrew $200.00. Remaining balance: $1300.00
The current balance for account 12345 is $1300.00.
Insufficient funds.
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, account_holder, initial_balance=0.0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account.

        :param account_number: Unique identifier for the account.
        :param account_holder: Name of the account holder.
        :param initial_balance: Starting balance of the account.
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.account_holder = account_holder
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit money into the account.

        :param amount: Amount to deposit.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited ${amount:.2f}. New balance: ${self.balance:.2f}")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw money from the account.

        :param amount: Amount to withdraw.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew ${amount:.2f}. Remaining balance: ${self.balance:.2f}")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check the balance of the account.
        """
        print(f"The current balance for account {self.account_number} is ${self.balance:.2f}.")

# Example usage:
# account = BankAccount(12345, "John Doe", 1000.0)
# account.check_balance()
# account.deposit(500.0)
# account.withdraw(200.0)
# account.withdraw(1500.0)  # Should trigger insufficient funds

Linear Regression and Trend Line with Std Dev Afl

Hi I am not sure if you are after this, this Afl has slope calculation in it..

Code:
``````_SECTION_BEGIN("Trend Lines");
p1 = Param("TL 1 Periods", 20, 5, 50, 1);
p2 = Param("TL 2 Periods", 5, 3, 25, 1);
TL1 = LinearReg(C, p1);
TL2 = EMA(TL1, p2);
Col1 = IIf(TL1 > TL2, ParamColor("TL Up Colour", colorBlue), ParamColor("TL Dn Colour", colorRed));
Plot(TL1, "TriggerLine 1", Col1, styleLine|styleThick|styleNoLabel);
Plot(TL2, "TriggerLine 2", Col1, styleLine|styleThick|styleNoLabel);
_SECTION_END();

_SECTION_BEGIN("Linear Regression Channel");
// CyberMan's Linear Regression Channel.. http://www.wisestocktrader.com/indicators/2690-price-with-regression-and-trend
// Linear Regression Line with 2 Standard Deviation Channels Plotted Above and Below
// The original was written by Patrick Hargus, with critical hints from Marcin Gorzynski, Amibroker.com Technical Support
// Wysiwyg coded the angle in degrees part
// I modified the original Linear Regression code so that the line will change color based on the degree of the Linear Regression slope.
// I combine this with my trading system.
// I will only take long positions if the Linear Regression line is green and the entry price is below the LR line.
// I will only take short positions if the Linear Regression line is red and the entry price is above the LR line.
// It is useful for filtering out lower probability trades.

//=====================Start Chart Configuration======================================

SetChartOptions(0,chartShowArrows|chartShowDates);
_N(Title = StrFormat("{{NAME}} - {{INTERVAL}} {{DATE}} Open %g, Hi %g, Lo %g, Close %g (%.1f%%) Vol " +WriteVal( V, 1.0 ) +" {{VALUES}}", O, H, L, C, SelectedValue( ROC( C, 1 )) ));
SetChartBkGradientFill(ParamColor("Top", colorTeal), ParamColor("Bottom", colorLightGrey), ParamColor("Title", colorTeal));
SetChartBkColor(colorTeal);

Plot( C, "Close", colorWhite, styleCandle, Zorder = 1);

//=====================End Chart Configuration========================================

//=====================Start of Linear Regression Code===================================

P = ParamField("Price field",-1);

Length = 3;

Daysback = Param("Period for Liner Regression Line",Length,1,240,1);
shift = Param("Look back period",0,0,240,1);

//===================== Math Formula =============================================

x = Cum(1);
lastx = LastValue( x ) - shift;
aa = LastValue( Ref(LinRegIntercept( p, Daysback), -shift) );
bb = LastValue( Ref(LinRegSlope( p, Daysback ), -shift) );
y = aa + bb * ( x - (Lastx - DaysBack +1 ) );

//=====================Plot the Linear Regression Line ===================================

LRColor = ParamColor("LR Color", colorCycle );

LRStyle = ParamStyle("LR Style");

// A slope higher than 0.05 radians will turn green, less than -0.05 will turn red and anything in between will be white.
Angle = Param("Angle", 0.05, 0, 1.5, 0.01);

LRLine = IIf( x > (lastx - Daysback) AND BarIndex() < Lastx, y, Null );

Pi = 3.14159265 * atan(1); // Pi
SlopeAngle = atan(bb)*(180/Pi);

LineUp = SlopeAngle > Angle;
LineDn = SlopeAngle < - Angle;

if(LineUp)
{
Plot(LRLine, "Lin. Reg. Line Up", IIf(LineUp, colorBrightGreen, colorWhite), LRStyle);
}
else
{
Plot(LRLine, "Lin. Reg. Line Down", IIf(LineDn, colorDarkRed, colorWhite), LRStyle);
}

Trend = IIf(LRLine > Ref(LRLine,-1),colorGreen,colorRed);//Changes LR line to green if sloping up and red if sloping down.

Plot( LRLine , "LinReg", Trend, LRSTYLE );

//======================Plot 1st SD Channel ========================================

SDP = Param("Standard Deviation", 1.5, 0, 6, 0.1);
SD = SDP/2;

width = LastValue( Ref(SD*StDev(p, Daysback),-shift) ); //Set width of inside chanels here.
SDU = IIf( x > (lastx - Daysback) AND BarIndex() < Lastx, y+width , Null ) ;
SDL = IIf( x > (lastx - Daysback) AND BarIndex() < Lastx, y-width , Null ) ;

SDColor = ParamColor("SD Color", colorCycle );
SDStyle = ParamStyle("SD Style");

Plot( SDU , "Upper Lin Reg", colorWhite,SDStyle ); //Inside Regression Lines
Plot( SDL , "Lower Lin Reg", colorWhite,SDStyle ); //Inside Regression Lines

//=====================  Plot 2d SD Channel ==========================================

SDP2 = Param("2d Standard Deviation", 2.0, 0, 6, 0.1);
SD2 = SDP2/2;

width2 = LastValue( Ref(SD2*StDev(p, Daysback),-shift) ); //Set width of outside chanels here.
SDU2 = IIf( x > (lastx - Daysback) AND BarIndex() < Lastx, y+width2 , Null ) ;
SDL2 = IIf( x > (lastx - Daysback) AND BarIndex() < Lastx, y-width2 , Null ) ;

SDColor2 = ParamColor("2 SD Color", colorCycle );
SDStyle2 = ParamStyle("2 SD Style");

Plot( SDU2 , "Upper Lin Reg", colorWhite,SDStyle2 ); //OutSide Regression Lines
Plot( SDL2 , "Lower Lin Reg", colorWhite,SDStyle2 ); //OutSide Regression Lines

//====================== End Indicator Code ==========================================``````
